Who May Inherit by Descent Under Intestate Laws (a) Natural Heirs 329 329 ... 330 1 Title acquired by will is really a subdivision of title by purchase according to Blackstone (see (12), but since the reason for maintaining the older classifications of Blackstone have long disappeared and as it seemed less confusing in the opinion of the author, to consider all title derived from decedents together, title by will has been made a subdivision of title by descent and will be so treated.

191. Modern Real Estate Business Now Regulated by Law. 

The business of an old-time conveyancer consisted for the most part of preparing legal documents relating to real estate, drawing deeds, mortgages and like instrument, etc., searching and making briefs or abstracts of title. The buying and selling of real estate was at the most a mere incident of his business. From the very nature of the business transacted the old-time conveyancer was a man of great learning. 

He studied faithfully and served a long apprenticeship before he essayed to start in business for himself. His knowledge of the technical points of conveyancing and the law of real estate was profound. Indeed, fifty years ago many of AM

§191 

the conveyancers were as well if not better versed in real estate law than most lawyers. A radical change has come over the business, due mainly to the rise of the title insurance companies, as set forth in the next chapter. In the larger cities, few, if any, real estate agents search title. About all the conveyancing that a modern real estate agent now does is drawing agreements of sale, instruments and leases. The greatest bulk, by far, of his business is buying, selling and renting real estate on commissions. He is more of a broker than a conveyancer, although that term is sometimes still used in conjunction with "agent" and "broker". It is the modern business we are interested in and, therefore, in this chapter we will treat of the law relating to real estate brokers and agents. The business of a real estate broker is now regulated by the Act of May 1, 1929 P.L. 1216, (63 P.S. 431 et seq.) which is cited as the Real Estate Brokers License Act of 1929. 

They may not transact business unless licensed thereunder. 

While real estate brokers prior to this Act of 1929 were also required to be licensed and were denied the right to sue for commissions if they were not licensed (Luce v. Cook, 227 Pa. 224-1910), no special qualifications to obtain a license were then required. All that was necessary was for a broker to pay the license tax imposed by various Acts of Assembly of which the last was May 7, 1907 P. L. 175 (72 P.S. 

2901 et. seq.) and this entitled him to a license from the 

County Treasurer. The ease with which anyone could set himself up in business as a real estate broker, regardless of qualifications, educational or moral, and regardless of knowledge, training or experience, was distinctly not in public interest. The lack of regulation by a supervising authority and the ease of obtaining licenses made possible many improper practices by persons utterly unfit to be real estate brokers and thus tended to bring the whole business into disrepute.

The realization of this condition led to the passage of the Real Estate Brokers Act of 1929 which ended the loose practice permitting any one by paying a license tax to obtain a

§ 

499 license.

It placed the issuance of licenses under the State 

Department of Public Instruction. Prescribed qualifications and a supervisory and regulatory power to deal with those guilty of improper practices. By this Act the business of a real estate broker has now been elevated to that of a profession which no one may practice unless he qualifies under the Act. Consequently its provisions and application will have our careful consideration in the next section (192). 

192. Real Estate Brokers License Act of 1929. 

The Real Estate Brokers License Act of 1929 (Act of May 1, 

1929 P. L. 1216 §1; 63 P.S. 431), is entitled "An Act to define 

Real Estate Brokers and Real Estate Salesmen; and providing for the licensing, regulation, and supervision of resident and non-resident real estate brokers and real estate salesmen and their business."

As above indicated the purpose of the Act is a comprehensive regulation of the business of selling real estate for others. To that end, sellers are divided into two general groups, one requiring a license and the other not. Each group is subdivided into classes; the first group into two classes, 

(a) Real Estate Brokers and (b) Real Estate Salesmen. The second group comprises those who are exempted from the provisions of the Act. The distinction between the two main groups depends on whether or not a party was engaged in the business of selling real estate for others; parties so engaged are in the first group. Parties not in the business of selling for others are in the second group. The duty of issuing licenses and otherwise administering the Statute is vested in the Department of Public Instruction. (Verona v. 

Schenley Farms Co. 312 Pa. 57-1933). 

(a) Parties Required to be Licensed Under the Act. 

1. Real Estate Brokers. All real estate brokers must be licensed under the Act and §2 thereof as most recently amended by the Act of July 2, 1937 P.L. 2811 (63 P.S. 432), comprehensively defines the term real estate broker as includ-

§192(a)  

ing "all persons, co-partnerships, associations and corporations foreign and domestic, who for another and for a fee, commission or other valuable consideration, shall sell, exchange, purchase or rent, or shall negotiate the sale, exchange, purchase or rental, or shall offer or attempt to negotiate the sale, exchange, purchase or rental, or shall hold himself or themselves out as engaged in the business of selling, exchanging, purchasing or renting of any real estate, interest in real estate, the property of another whether the same shall be located within the State of Pennsylvania or elsewhere, or shall collect or offer or attempt to collect rental for the use of real estate, property of another or shall negotiate or offer or attempt to negotiate a loan secured or to be secured by a mortgage or other encumbrance upon or transfer of any real estate. It includes also any person, co-partnership association or corporation, employed by the owner or owners of lots or other parcels of real estate including cemetery lots, at a stated salary or upon commission or upon a salary and commission basis or otherwise, to sell such real estate or any part thereof in lots or parcels, who shall sell or exchange or offer or attempt to negotiate the sale or exchange of any such lot or parcel of real estate." The term "broker" under the act as amended also includes "persons, co-partnerships, associations and corporations foreign and domestic, the principal business of whom or which, is the purchasing, selling, renting, trading or exchanging of real estate, whether for another or as a purchaser, seller or owner of such real estate, and all real estate appraisers, auctioneers and all persons who negotiate or offer for sale any mortgage or other security for which real estate is collateral as well as all managers of office buildings, apartment buildings and persons employed by banking institutions or trust companies for the foregoing purposes.' 

It should be noted also that by the express language of subdivision (a) of §2 of the law "One act in consideration of compensation, by fee, commission or otherwise, of buying, selling, renting or exchanging any such real estate of or for another, or attempting or offering to do so, or negotiating a

§(b)  

501 loan upon or leasing or renting or placing for rent any such real estate, or collection of rent therefrom, shall constitute prima facie evidence that the person, copartnership, association or corporation so acting or attempting to act, is a real estate broker within the meaning of this Act." This provision works a material change in the law for prior thereto one transaction or even an occasional one did not constitute the negotiator a broker if selling or buying real estate for others was not his principal business (Woods v. Heron 229 Pa. 

625-1911; Clark v. Freeport etc. Co. 52 Pa. Super. Ct. 11912). Now a single transaction subjects a person to the penalties imposed by the Act of 1929 (Burns v. Gartzman 139 

Pa. Super. Ct. 453—1939), unless he can bring himself within one of the classes of the group exempted by the provisions of subdivision (c) of §2 of Act (63 P.S. 432 (c)) discussed in the next subsection of this book (b) infra.; and the burden of so proving is on him (Verona v. Schenley Farms Co. 312 Pa. 57-1933). 

2. Real Estate Salesmen. Subdivision (b) of §2 of the Act (63 P.S. 432 (b)) defines a Real Estate Salesman as follows: 

"Real Estate Salesman" shall mean and include any person employed by a licensed Real Estate Broker to perform any or all of the functions of a real estate broker. Provided however, that stenographic or other clerical help, employed solely in such capacity in the office of the broker, shall not be required to be licensed as a real estate salesman." 

(b) Persons Excepted from the Provisions of the Act. 

Subdivision (c) of §2 as amended (63 P.S. 432 (c)) specifies who and under what conditions certain parties are excepted from the provisions of, or application to them of the Act. 

These are 1. Owners of real estate dealing with their own property where trading in real estate is not the principal business of such owner. 

2. A person holding in good faith a letter of attorney from the actual owner of real estate authorizing the sale, con-

§192 (b)  

veyance or leasing thereof, if only one transaction is involved and if the letter of attorney is recorded in the Recorder of Deeds' office. By letter of attorney is meant power of attorney constituting the person named as attorney in fact for the owner with authority to sell, convey, lease or negotiate in the name of the owner (Freeman v. Foster 121 Pa. Super. Ct. 595-1936). 

3. Attorneys at law are exempt from provisions of the Act. 

It has been ruled by the Department of Justice the exemption of an attorney at law, entitles him to act as an agent for a corporation without obtaining a broker's or salesman's license even though the chief business of that corporation is the sale of real estate (Attorneys as Real Estate Brokers 39 

D. & C. 42-1940. Opin. by Deputy Atty. Gen. Barco). See also Young v. Department of Pub. Instruc. (105 Pa. Super. Ct. 

153, 159—1932) where it is pointed out that attorneys at law have carried on real estate transactions as a part of their professional duties performed for clients for many years. 

That they are admitted to the bar only after having established their qualifications and good moral character and are responsible to the court for their good conduct. They are not within the class at which the Statute was aimed because they had not been the source of the mischief sought to be remedied. Such exemption was there held not to render the 

Real Estate Broker's Act of 1929 unconstitutional. 

4. Justices of Peace and Aldermen, during their term of office. By the Supplementary Act of May 26, 1939 P.L. 230 

(63 P.S. 436.1) Justices of Peace and Aldermen who are engaged in the real estate business on its effective date may continue in such business after the expiration of their term of office provided they shall meet all the requisites of applicants for real estate brokers license except they shall not be required to have served in the employ of a duly licensed real estate broker as licensed real estate salesman for 2 years. 

5. "Receivers" and "Trustees in Bankruptcy" “Administrators, Executors or other Person or Corporation Acting

§192(c)  

503 under Court Appointment," or "Trustee under Will or Deed of Trust" are exempt "where only the transactions pertaining thereto are involved". 

6. Elected Executive Officers of any banking institution or trust company operating under the banking laws of Pennsylvania.

In Comm. v. Kleckner 14 Leh. 163–1931, 79 Pitts. L.J. 2721931, it was held the Real Estate Brokers License Act of 1929 (63 P.S. 431) was not unconstitutional because it exempted attorneys and Justices of Peace since both being officers of the Court are within the Court's supervision at all times. 

(See also J. A. Young & Co. v. Dept. of Pub. Instruction 105 

Pa. Super. Ct. 153-1932). 

(c) How Licenses May be Obtained. Applications. Quali fications.

The Act as first passed in 1929 in order to visit no hardships upon persons then in the real estate business allowed them, if of good repute, to obtain licenses upon filing an application on forms supplied by the Department accompanied by recommendations of at least two owners of real estate certifying to the honesty, truthfulness and good repute of the applicant, or where the applicant was a corporation, copartnership or association then, of its officers or members. 

Since 1935 however, examinations for licenses of both real estate brokers and salesmen are required and now the procedure for obtaining such licenses is briefly as follows. 

1. Broker's Applications and Qualifications. An applicant for a broker's license must possess the following qualifications. He, (if an individual) or if a corporation, copartnership or association, then all the officers or members thereof, who are actively engaged in the real estate business, must be 21 years of age or upwards; citizens of the United States and must have served an apprenticeship as duly licensed real estate salesmen of not less than 2 years in the employ of a duly licensed broker or brokers. (§6 as amended, 63 P.S. 436.)

They must also pass an examination as hereinafter noted (see

§192 (d)  

sub. sec. (d) infra.). Applications must be made in writing upon blanks furnished by the department, signed by the applicant and must be accompanied by recommendations of at least two owners of real estate, not related to the applicant, and residents of the County where applicant resides or has his place of business, certifying to the honesty, truthfulness and good repute of the applicant or of the officers or members if the applicant is a corporation, copartnership or association. 

If the applicant has been in business or has resided less than a year in the County from which he applies, then similar recommendations of at least two real estate owners (not related to the applicant), of the County or Counties where the applicant formerly resided or engaged in business, must also be filed. The application must be accompanied by two photographs of the applicant or of the officers or members if the applicant is a corporation, copartnership or association. 

Branch Offices. A duplicate license must be procured for each branch office maintained by the applicant for which an additional charge of $2.50 for each additional office must be paid¹ (§7 (b) as amended 63 P.S. 437 (b)). 

2. Salesmens Applications and Qualifications. Application for a salesman's license must also be in writing on a blank furnished by the department and set forth inter alia, the period of time during which he has been engaged in business, the name of the last employer and name of the employer or the corporation, copartnership or association employing him or whose employ he is then about to enter. The application must be accompanied by the recommendation of his employer or prospective employer certifying to applicant's honesty, truthfulness, good repute and recommending that such license be granted (§7 (c) as amended; 63 P.S. 437 (c)). 

(d) Examinations. 

No license may now issue to an applicant for a broker's or salesman's license unless he passes an examination conducted 

1 For other fees to be paid see infra. (h).

§192(e)  

505 by the Department except in case of nonresident brokers as hereinafter noted. The broker's examination must "include questions on real estate practice, real estate law, the applicant's ability to read and write English, his knowledge of mathematics." A salesman's examination is limited to the common school branches (§7 (c) as amended by Act of July 2, 1937 P.L. 2811, §3 (63 P.S. 437 (c)). The examinations are re quired to be held on the second Saturdays of January, April, July and October at Philadelphia, Pittsburgh, Harrisburg,

Wilkesbarre and Erie at the hours and under the rules prescribed by the Department. (id.) 

An individual may obtain a temporary permit to operate as a salesman until the next regular examination. If he fails to pass at two successive examinations, he shall be ineligible to take a similar examination for two years thereafter. The word "similar" has been ruled to mean that after the two year period only one examination may be taken and not two. (Real 

Estate Brokers' Examinations, 39 D. & C. 695—1940.) 

If a real estate broker or salesman has his license cancelled by the department no new license may be issued to him unless he complies with the Act. This would seem to include the examination requirement (§7 (c) as amended 63 P.S. 437 (c)). (e) Nonresidents.

Nonresidents may obtain a broker's or salesman's license upon complying with the provisions of the Act relative to residents. (§7 (e) 63 P.S. 437 (e)). But where the nonresident applicant is a broker or a salesman holding a license as such from the State of his domicile, the Department may accept in lieu of the recommendations and statements otherwise required to accompany the application, a duly certified copy of the license issued to such nonresident by the state of his domicile (§7 (f) 63 P.S. 437 (f)). The department may in such case, also waive the examination requirements if reciprocal courtesies are extended to the brokers and salesmen of this State. ($7 (c) as amended, 63 P.S. 437 (c)). 

Non resident licensees who are licensed brokers in the State of their domicile and maintain an active place of business

§192(f) 

there, need not maintain an office in this State if reciprocal courtesies are extended by that State to this. However in such case the nonresident licensed broker must file with the department an irrevocable consent to be bound by process issued out of the court of any County in suits against him, served on the Department. (§7, (f) 63 P.S. 437 (f)). 

(f) Corporation, Copartnership and Association License 

Restrictions.

Corporations, Associations (by which is probably meant limited registered and partnership associations, see supra. §41 (b) (c) and (d) of this book) and copartnerships may obtain real estate broker's licenses, but the authority to transact business thereunder is restricted to one officer, member or one partner who will be named in the license. Other officers, members or partners who desire to act as real estate brokers in connection with the business of the firm, association or company, must take out separate licenses individually (§7 (d) as amended, 63 P.S. 437 (d)). 

(g) Duration of Licenses When Issued. 

Broker's and Salesmen's licenses expire Dec. 31 of the year issued, unless sooner revoked or suspended by the Department or renewed annually as in the next sub. sec. (h) described. (§7 (b) and (c) as amended; 63 P.S. 437 b and c). (h) Fees for Licenses and Renewals. Tax on Gross Receipts. 

1. Broker's License and Renewal Fees. A fee of $10.00 must accompany an application for examination for a real estate broker's license. If he passes, no additional fee is required for his license. If he fails to pass, he is eligible to take the next examination without any additional fee. Where a broker maintains more than one office or place of business he must pay an additional license fee of $2.50 annually for each additional office or place of business ($7 (b) as amended; 

63 P.S. 437 (b)). A real estate broker must register annually by making application for a renewal of his license on or

$(h)  

507 before January 1st of the next succeeding year and pay the sum of $5.00 for such renewal. (§8 (3) of the Act as amended; 

63 P.S. 438 (3)). 

2. Salesmen License and Renewal Fees. The application for examination for a real estate salesman's license must be accompanied by a fee of $5.00. If the applicant passes, no additional fee is required for issuance of his certificate. If he fails, he is eligible to take the next examination without any additional fee. As in the case of the broker a salesman must register annually by applying for a renewal to the department annually on or before January 1st of the next succeeding year and pay for such renewal $2.50 (§8 (3) as amended; 63 P.S. 

438 (3)). 

3. Registration Lists. The Department is required to publish annually a list of all persons, co-partnerships and corporations registered for that particular year (§8, (4) as amended; 63 P.S. 438-(4) anno.). 

4. Tax on Gross Receipts. It must be noted that a real estate broker must still pay the license tax imposed by the Act of May 7, 1907 P.L. 175 as amended by the 

Act of April 25, 1929 P.L. 687 (72 P.S. 2901 et seq.) which requires all real estate brokers, etc., to pay an annual license tax on their gross annual receipts and commissions etc. as follows: $10 on less than $5,000; $25 in excess of $5,000 and less than $10,000; $50 on $10,000 and less than $20,000; and $100 by those whose gross receipts are $20,000 and upwards. This license tax must be paid in addition to the license fees specified in the Real Estate Broker's License Act of 1929, §8, as amended (63 P.S. 438) for that Act was held in Newhouse v. Dipner (118 Pa. Super. Ct. 101-1935) not to have repealed the Act of 1907 and its amendment. But the payment of the license tax under the Act of 1907 as amended is no longer a condition precedent to the maintenance of a suit to collect a commission by a real estate broker duly licensed under Real Estate Broker's License Law of 

1929 Newhouse v. Dipner (Supra.).

§192(i)  

This license tax under the Act of 1907 as amended is assessed by the mercantile appraisers who distribute annually blanks supplied by the Department of Revenue of the Commonwealth, which the broker is required to fill out and set forth the volume of business he did during the preceding year. Upon the basis of this "return" the mercantile appraisers assess the tax which must then be paid to the County Treasurer, (Act of May 7, 1907 P.L. 175 as amended by Act of Apr. 25, 1929 P.L. 687, §§1 to 5; 72 P.S. 2910 to 2915). 

(i) Place of Business, Name, Display of Licenses, Notice of Change. 

1. Real Estate Brokers. The licensed real estate brokers must maintain a definite place of business within the Commonwealth which shall be a room or rooms used exclusively for the real estate business or such business and any allied business. The place of business must be designated in the license and the certificate of registration and annual renewal card must be properly displayed at such place. Business may not be transacted at any other place. Ten days notice of change of location of office must be given to the department who will thereupon provide a new license for the new location without charge. The name Real Estate or Realtor¹ must be displayed outside of the place of business. The department, subject to the approval of the Real Estate Advisory Committee, may adopt reasonable rules and regulations for the conduct of such places of business. (§9 (a) as amended; 63 

P.S. 439 (a)). Non resident licensed brokers, who are also licensed in the State of their domicile and who meet the conditions mentioned in sub. sec. (e) (Supra.) need not comply with this provision of the Act. 

2. Salesmen. The certificate of registration and the cur rent annual renewal card of all salesmen employed by the broker, must be prominently displayed in the broker's place or places of business. The license issued to the real estate 

1 The word Realtor is restricted to use of members of Real Estate Boards and may be used only by their members.

§(j)  

509 salesmen, must designate the employer of such salesman by name. Ten days' notice in writing must be given to the department by the salesman and by the broker when the salesman changes his employer, whereupon the department will issue a new license with the name of the new employer, for the unexpired term without charge. Failure of a real estate salesman to notify the department of change of employment automatically cancels the license to him, (§9 (b) 63 P.S. 

439 (b)). 

3. The violation of any of these provisions is cause for suspension or revocation of the license in the discretion of the department. §9 (c) of the Act as amended; (63 P.S. 

439 (c)). 

(j) Practices Prohibited by the Act for Which Licenses 

May be Suspended or Revoked. 

1. §10 (a), as amended (63 P.S. 440 (a)) prohibits in express terms certain practices which would tend to bring the real estate profession into disrepute, as well as unwise business conduct that would tend to be destructive of the best interests of the brokers and salesmen. These practices as enumerated and set forth in the above section of the Act, make it unlawful and punishable by suspension or revocation of license for a broker or salesman. 

1. To knowingly make any substantial misrepresentation. 

2. To knowingly make any false promise of a character likely to influence, persuade or induce. 

3. To be guilty of a continued or flagrant course of misrepresentation or making of false promises through agents or salesmen. 

4. When it shall be shown that the licensee, within five years prior to the issuance of the license has been convicted in a court of this or any other State of forgery, embezzlement, obtaining money under false pretenses, extortion, conspiracy to defraud, or other like offense or offenses; the license is revocable.

§192 (j)  

5. To fail to account for, or to pay over any moneys belonging to others which has come into the broker's or salesman's possession arising out of a real estate transaction. 

6. To make any misleading or untruthful advertisement. 

7. To do any act or conduct in connection with real estate transactions which demonstrate incompetency, bad faith, or dishonesty. 

8. To display a "For Rent" or "For Sale" sign on any property without an exclusive agency therefor or without the owner's consent. 99 In addition to the above it should be noted that a conviction of a broker or salesman in a court of competent jurisdiction of this or any other State, of "forgery", "embezzlement", "obtaining money under false pretenses," "extortion,' 'criminal conspiracy to defraud" or other like offense or offenses when duly certified and filed with the department, requires the department to forthwith revoke the license of the convicted licensee. (§11 (b); 63 P.S. 441 (b)).

So also the indictment of a licensee in this or any other State, for offenses enumerated in the foregoing paragraph, warrants the department in its discretion, upon receipt of a certified copy of the indictment, to suspend the license pending the trial upon such an indictment. ($11 (c); 63 P.S. 

441 (c)). 

When Broker's License May be Revoked for his Salesman's

Violation. A violation of any of the provisions of the Act by a real estate salesman or other employee of the licensed broker shall not be ground for revocation of the license of the employer unless it appears upon hearing that the employer had guilty knowledge of the violation or a course of dealing is shown to have been persistently and consistently followed by such employee which shall constitute prima facie evidence of knowledge on the part of the employer. $14 of the Act (63 P.S. 444).

§(k)  

511 

Sharing Commissions with Non Licensees Prohibited. It is expressly made unlawful for a licensed real estate broker or salesman to pay any compensation or other valuable thing to any one other than a licensed real estate broker or salesman for the rendering of any service or the doing of any act which is forbidden by the Real Estate Brokers License Act of 1929 to be rendered or performed by any other than licensees. For doing so his license may be suspended or revoked (§15 (a); 63 

P.S. 445 (a)).

Salesman May Receive Compensation or Commissions from 

Employer Only. So also a licensed salesman is forbidden to accept or receive compensation of any kind from any person other than a licensed real estate broker by whom he is employed, for any service rendered for work done by such salesman in buying, selling, exchanging, leasing or negotiating a loan upon real estate or interest therein. (§15 (b); 63 P.S. 

445 (b)). The above violations by a licensee is made sufficient cause for suspension or revocation of his license in the discretion of the department (id.). 

(k) Proceedings to Enforce Observance of the Law. 

1. Proceedings by the Department. The Department is given power to investigate, upon its own motion, and must, upon verified complaint in writing, setting forth specifically wrongful act or acts complained of on the part of a licensee, investigate the transaction. If after a hearing pursuant to notice to accused licensee, the Department finds him guilty of any of the foregoing enumerated prohibited practices, it may temporarily suspend or permanently revoke his license. 

Section 10 (b) of the Act of 1929 (63 P.S. 440 (b)) prescribes in detail how the proceedings and hearing are to be initiated and conducted. It includes a requirement that the hearing be fixed after notification to the accused licensee of not less than 10 days with the charges and copy of complaint against him.

If the Department dismisses the complaint, its decision is

§ 192 (1) 

final. Where however it makes a ruling adverse to the licensee, an appeal lies to the Dauphin County Court. 

When a decision is made adverse to the licensee, it becomes effective as of the date fixed by the Department in a notice thereof to the licensee, if not theretofore appealed from. The date fixed not to be less than 30 days from date of notice (§10 (d) as amended; 63 P.S. 440 (d)). 

2. Proceedings on Appeal to Court. When an appeal is taken to the Dauphin County Court it acts as a supersedeas until ten days from and after the filing of the transcript by the department in the Court of Common Pleas of Dauphin County and notice thereof given to the licensee, but no longer unless that Court shall upon motion of the licensee grant an order of supersedeas ($10 (e); 63 P.S. 440 (e)). The Act should be examined for the details of practice and proceedings, together with the case of Young v. Dept. of Public Instruction (105 Pa. Super. Ct. 153-1932), where the power of the department under this section is discussed and this portion of the Act held constitutional. 

(1) Effect of Proceedings to Revoke and of Revocation of Licenses. 

1. Effect of Pending Proceedings. Pending the termina tion of an investigation, proceeding or appeal, no new license will be issued to the licensee or to the partnership, association or corporation with which he is connected except for the period of such investigation. (§11 (e); 63 P.S. 441 (e)). 

2. Effect of Revocation of Licenses. After revocation of any license no new license may be issued to the same licensee within a period of one year from the date of the revocation nor within five years after a conviction of forgery, embezzlement, obtaining money under false pretenses, extortion, criminal conspiracy to defraud or other like offense or offenses, nor to any copartnership of which such person is a member nor to any copartnership, association or corporation of which the person guilty is a member, officer or employee or in which as a stockholder he exercises a controlling interest directly

§513 

or indirectly, nor at any time except in the sole discretion of the department, (§11 (f), (g); 63 P.S. 441 (f), (g)). And it of course follows a new application must be made and examinations taken and passed (sub. sec. (d) of this section supra.). 

3. Effect of Revocation of License of an Officer or Member of a Corporation, Co-Partnership or Association. Where a license of any member of a copartnership or of any officer of the association or corporation is revoked or suspended, the license to the firm, association or corporation shall also be revoked by the department unless within a time fixed, by the department, connection with the copartnership, association or corporation of the licensee whose license has been revoked or suspended, has been severed (§11, (d); 63 P.S. 441 (d)). 

(m) Miscellaneous Provisions of the Act. 

The duty of issuing licenses and of supervising and controlling the same is committed to the Department of Public Instruction of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania herein referred to as "Department" (§3; 63 P.S. 433). Copies of all records on file in the Department including stenographic notes of testimony when certified under seal of the Department, are made admissible in evidence subject to the Court's right to rquire production of the original (§4; 63 P.S. 434). 

The Secretary of the Department and all lesser officials and employees are forbidden to divulge information contained in the records of the department except as elsewhere provided in the Act and except where the production is required in a court proceeding, but after hearing, the rulings or decisions of the department with the record relative thereto upon which the same is founded, is made a public record and open to public inspection (§5; 63 P.S. 435). 

193. Effect of Not Obtaining a License. Penalties. 

Prior to the Real Estate Brokers License Act of 1929 (63 

P.S. 431 et seq.), there was no prohibition against engaging in the business of real estate broker or agent as it was fre-

$193 

quently called. The only disadvantage or penalty, if it could be so called, was that a broker could not recover his commissions if compelled to sue therefor. Now by the express terms of the Act, it is declared "to be unlawful for any person, copartnership, association or corporation to engage or carry on the business or even act in the capacity of a real estate broker or real estate salesman within the Commonwealth without first obtaining a license" as such from the 

Department (§6; 63 P.S. 436). 

Moreover it is now a criminal offence to do so, for $12 of the Act as amended (63 P.S. 442) makes it a "misdemeanor" to engage in or carry on the business or act in the capacity of a real estate broker or a real estate salesman within this Commonwealth without a license. So also it is a misdemeanor to continue business after suspension or revocation of a license, or to employ a person as a real estate salesman to whom no license has been issued or whose license was revoked or suspended. These offenses are punishable by a fine, for the first offense, and for any second or subsequent offense, by a greater fine or imprisonment or both in the discretion of the Court. It is also by the same section, made a lesser offense punishable by summary conviction for a holder of a certificate of registration who fails to apply for a renewal thereof, to continue to practice as broker or a salesman thereafter.

The former penalty of denying an unlicensed broker the right to sue for commissions due him is continued by §16 of the Act (63 P.S. 446), which declares that "no action or suit shall be instituted nor recovery therein had, in any Court of this Commonwealth by any person, copartnership, association or corporation, for the compensation for any act done or services rendered, the doing or rendering of which is prohibited under the provisions of this act to others than licensed real estate brokers, unless such person, copartnership, association or corporation was duly licensed hereunder as a real estate broker at the time of the doing of such act or the rendering of such service." See Verona v. Shenley Farms Co. (312 Pa. 

57-1933) construing this section and holding it constitutional.

§515 

Prior to this Act if a person was not in the business of and did not hold himself out as a broker he could recover on a special contract for services rendered in securing a purchaser or lessee of real estate even though he had no license (See 

Downing v. Marks 318 Pa. 289-1935) where the transaction took place before the Act of 1929, but the law is now definitely otherwise (Freeman v. Foster 121 Pa. Super. Ct. 595—1936; 

Burns v. Gartzman, 139 Pa. Super. Ct. 453—1939). 

194. When a Broker Is Entitled to Commissions. 

When and whether a broker has earned his commission, depends upon the consideration of two factors: (a) the terms of his employment and (b) whether he has performed the services he contracted to render. The second factor necessarily depends upon the first and much difficulty can be avoided if the terms of the contracts of employment are clearly stipulated. 

(a) The Contract of Employment. 

We must start with the proposition that no commissions are recoverable unless the broker has first been employed. 

A mere volunteer is not entitled to commissions though he bring the parties together (Lanard et al. v. Thompson Printing Co. 84 Pa. Super. Ct. 199-1924). There must be an employment to constitute the broker an agent (Lamb v. Elder 

56 Pa. Super. Ct. 522-1914; Earp v. Cummins 54 Pa. 3941867). The broker must show his employment either by previous authority or by the acceptance and adoption of his acts (Zellner v. Murdoch, 298 Pa. 208-1929). When a broker claims to have been employed by a corporation the burden is on him also to show that the official or employee of the corporation with whom he dealt, had the corporate authority to employ him (Lehan v. Integrity Trust Co. 303 Pa. 357-1931).

But the authority of a corporate officer to employ a broker on behalf of his corporation may be implied if the transaction is within the line of ordinary duties imposed on such officer 

(Kelly Murray Inc. v. Lansdowne Bank & T. Co. 299 Pa. 236 

-1930).

§194(a)  

The necessity for a contract, express or implied, cannot be supplied by pleading a mere custom or usage. Nor can one broker recover a share of commissions from another by proof of a rule of a real estate board (of which neither is a member), to the effect that where a real estate broker produces a client to another broker and the sale is consummated, the commission is to be divided. (Hagedorn & Co. v. Godwin and Strauss, Inc., 142 Pa. Super. Ct. 547--1940). 

1. Employment by Fiduciaries. Where a broker deals with co-executors or co-trustees, his employment must be by all for they must act as a unit. (Morley v. Carson, 240 Pa. 546 

-1913; Benezet v. Hess 24 D.R. 776-1915). It has been recently held that where a broker deals with a fiduciary his right to commissions must be considered as subject to a condition implied by law that the fiduciary's acceptance of the purchaser is subject to the approval of the Orphans' Court, so that the commissions are not to be considered earned if the Orphans' Court refuses to confirm the sale (Clark v. 

Provident Trust Co. Tr. 329 Pa. 421-1938). 

2. Need Not be in Writing Unless Broker is to Sign Agreement of Sale. It is a good practice both for the broker and the property owner, to put every contract of employment in writing though a contract is none the less binding if it be oral. If, however, it is intended to authorize the broker to sign the principal's name to an agreement of sale such authority must be in writing because of the Statute of Frauds (see $46 Supra. of this book). A broker who without written authority undertakes to sign an agreement of sale, makes himself personally liable to the purchaser, (Krewson v. Fisher, 78 Pa. Super. Ct. 509—1922). A broker's contract of employment may even be implied from certain circumstances as e.g. where an owner lists his property with a broker for sale; or from an exchange of telegrams or correspondence as in 

Gibbons v. Monongahela R. Co. (68 Pa. Super. Ct. 232—1917). 

But employment will not be implied from the mere fact that the broker received a postal card, sent as well to many other

§194(b)  

517 brokers, that the owner had a property for rent, this being considered a mere notice (Lanard et al. v. Thompson Printing 

Co. 84 Pa. Super. Ct. 199-1924).

When the contract is in writing, its terms of course govern and fix the nature of the services required to be rendered in order to entitle the broker to his commission. The different varieties of broker's contracts usually met with will be discussed from the standpoint of their effect on the question; 

When are broker's commissions considered to have been earned? 

(b) Nature and extent of Services Necessary. 

1. Ordinary Contract of Employment. When the broker's contract of employment is the ordinary or customary one, whereby the broker is employed to secure a purchaser, he is considered as having earned his commission when he procures a party with whom his principal is satisfied, or who actually contracts for the property at a price satisfactory to the owner without reference to the outcome of the transaction 

(Lieberman et al. v. Colahan 267 Pa. 102-1920; Mearkle & 

Bros. v. Kellerman 82 Pa. Super. Ct. 11-1923). In such case it makes no difference why the agreement of sale is never carried out. Whether because the purchaser refuses to make settlement (McKinney et al. v. Smith 85 Pa. Super. Ct. 5251925; Hipple v. Laird 189 Pa. 472-1899; Folsom v. Slattery, 

54 Pa. Super. Ct. 350-1913); or because vendor's wife refuses to join in the deed (Noon v. Rodstein 89 Pa. Super. Ct. 

153-1926); or because parties in interest other than the seller, refuse to join in the sale (Aber v. Pennsylvania Co. 269 

Pa. 384-1921); or because the owner, through no fault of his own, is unable to secure a release of encumbrance so as to convey the kind of title agreed on (Irons v. Snyder 49 Pa. 

Super. Ct. 522-1912). For in all these instances the broker has earned his commissions when he produced a purchaser whom the owner accepted or with whom he contracted. 

So also the broker has earned his commission if he produces to his principal, a person ready, able and willing to contract

§194 (b) 

at the price fixed by his principal, notwithstanding the refusal of the principal to sign the agreement of sale (Wilson v. Hays 

283 Pa. 271-1925; Burchfield v. Griffith, 10 Pa. Super Ct. 

618-1899; Miller v. Kenneck 20 D. R. 706-1911), for in such case the broker has done what he contracted to do, namely, procure a purchaser.

The broker's services however must be the efficient cause of the sale (Bossart v. Erie Co. etc. 276 Pa. 63—1923). Yet where a sale has resulted from the efforts of the broker, even though they amounted to a mere introduction of the property to the buyer (Lamb v. Elder 56 Pa. Super. Ct. 522-1914), or brought to the buyer's attention by advertisement of the broker or by his sign (Peters v. Holmes 45 Pa. Super. Ct. 

278-1911), he is, nevertheless, entitled to his commissions (Keys v. Johnson 68 Pa. 42-1871). An owner cannot while negotiations are pending by his broker with a purchaser, take it in his own hands and complete the sale either above or below the price first mentioned and refuse to pay commission (Warne v. Johnston 48 Pa. Super. Ct. 98-1911; Peters v. Holmes, Supra.; Sowney v. Bair, 269 Pa. 448-1921). Nor can the owner under such circumstances, conclude the sale through a second broker and escape liability to the first, and the fact that by so doing, he renders himself liable to pay two commissions, is no defense, (Girsh v. Rolland, 285 Pa. 141—1926).

In other words as picturesquely put in Davis v. Clark (25 

D. R. 35-1915), "after one broker has shaken the tree another cannot be permitted to run up and carry off the fruit; and if the owner permits this to be done he is responsible to the first agent".

The effect and the principles to be gleaned from these decisions have been well summarized by Judge Patton in his charge to the jury in Thompson v. Goldman (51 Pa. Super. 

Ct. 632-1912), as follows: "Brokers in order to recover their commissions must show (a) that they had procured for the defendant a party with whom he was satisfied and who actually contracted for the property at a price satisfactory to the owner; or (b) that it was through their efforts that the

§194(b) 

519 purchaser was secured to whom the defendant, after he wrongfully took the matter out of their hands, made the sale; or (c) that even if they had not secured any binding contract from the proposed purchaser, they had actually produced to their principal a purchaser able and willing to perform his part of the proposal so that failure to complete an actual sale resulted alone from the fault of the principal." 

Computation of Commissions. Where a broker effects a sale of property for a sum named he is entitled to his commission on that sum irrespective of the fact that there is a mortgage on the property (Peters v. Holmes 45 Pa. Super. Ct. 278-1911).

Commissions to Secure Mortgage Loans or Tenants. In general it may be said that the law relating to broker's commissions for securing mortgage loans is governed by the same principles as those relating to sales (Middleton v. Thompson 

163 Pa. 112-1894). Where a broker is employed to negotiate a loan, he is entitled to his commission when he brings to his principal a party ready and willing to take the loan and the principal cannot escape liability by refusing to accept the loan (Mearkle Bros. v. Kellerman 82 Pa. Super. Ct. 111923); and where the broker has fully performed his part, he is entitled to his commission, notwithstanding the death of the borrower prevented the loan transaction from being completed (Furbush's Estate 16 D.R. 205-1907). 

Rental Commissions. Commissions for securing tenants are also in general governed by the same principles. (See 

Heyman v. Fourth Dickerson Bldg. & Loan Assn. 113 Pa. 

Super. Ct. 26-1934; Lanard et al. v. Thompson Printing Co. 

84 Pa. Super. Ct. 199—1924). 

2. Special Contracts for Commissions. 

The authorities previously considered all related to the ordinary contract, where the broker is employed to secure a purchaser, and we have seen that in such case the broker's right to a commission accrues as soon as he presents a pur-

§194 (b)2 

chaser, ready, able and willing to purchase the property on agreed terms. However this is so only in absence of an agreement to the contrary (Clark v. Provident Trust Co. Tr. 

329 Pa. 421–1938). It is entirely within the province of an owner of real estate to specify in his contract employing a broker, a condition that payment of commissions is only to be made upon actual settlement and full payment of the purchase price by the purchaser. Such condition, if inserted, will be upheld (Clark v. Provident Trust Tr. Co. Supra.), for there is no rule of law or public policy which prevents a real estate broker agreeing to make the payment of his commission absolutely dependent upon a settlement of the transaction. 

Thus in (Swotes v. Schneider 129 Pa. Super. Ct. 214-1937) where the broker expressly agreed that no commission was to be paid him if for any reason settlement was not made by the purchaser or the seller, it was held commissions were not recoverable even though the fault was that of the seller. 

Where, however, the condition imposed in a special contract for commissions, merely provides that the commissions were to be paid "on settlement by your purchaser" and the owner subsequently cancelled the contract and returned the hand money, it was held that as settlement could never be made because of the owner's own act, the broker became entitled to his commissions (Aber v. Pennsylvania Co. 269 Pa. 

384-1921). In Greenblatt v. Fox (59 Pa. Super. Ct. 531915) the special contract read "on completion of transaction entered into with you this day I agree to pay you 1% on the purchase money of the house, that is the sum of $575.” 

The purchaser never completed the settlement and the deposit money paid by him was forfeited. It was held to have been the duty of the owner to make demand upon the purchaser to complete his contract and do something in affirmance thereof before he could deny the broker his commission. 

In making special contracts for the payment of commissions, both owners and brokers will do well to avoid the use of equivocal terms such as e. g. "commissions to be paid at settlement". Such clause might mean either that com-

§194(b) 

521 missions are dependent upon the purchaser making settlement or that payment is merely postponed until settlement. 

Under the former construction, the broker might lose his commissions if his purchaser did not settle. Under the latter construction his commissions might be considered as earned for only the time of the payment was postponed and in such case, if the purchaser refuses to make settlement, the commissions may nevertheless become due (Simon v. Myers 284 

Pa. 3-1925). How such equivocal language is to be construed depends upon the construction of the whole agreement in the light of the circumstances as in e. g. Kaiser v. Grossman (87 Pa. Super. Ct. 421-1926), where the former construction was made because the broker was only to be paid "any sum which the seller received in excess of $4,000 at the settlement". However in Amorosa v. Ovelman (89 Pa. 

Super. Ct. 377-1926) a special written contract read that the broker was to receive $100. as commission "at settlement" and it was there held that oral testimony might be introduced to show the circumstances under which the words, ambiguous in meaning, were understood by the parties. 

It seems the general tendency of the cases is to construe the ambiguous language in favor of the broker's right to be paid his commission where the settlement fails to take place through no fault of his and where the seller makes no attempt to enforce his contract against the purchaser (Simon v. Myers 284 Pa. 3-1925). See also McKinney et al. v. 

Smith (85 Pa. Super. Ct. 525—1925), where the commissions were to be paid "for bringing about a sale". But see also 

Matuszewski v. Grisius (118 Pa. Super. Ct. 196-1935). 

3. Exclusive Agency Contracts. Form. In the form of the 

"special contract" just considered the property owner sought the advantage of requiring the broker to go without his commission unless settlement actually took place. The so called exclusive agency contracts go to the other extreme and as drawn, frequently seek to compel a property owner to pay commission to a broker regardless of the fact whether he or someone else brings about the sale. Here again the position

§194(b)3 

of the Courts has been that where the contract clearly expresses the terms it will be enforced. So in Owens v. Wehrle (14 Pa. Super. Ct. 536-1900), the special contract stipulated that the broker employed should receive a commission if the property recited, be sold within a stipulated time, either by the broker or any other person, and it was held the broker was entitled to recover his commission even in absence of proof that he made any effort to sell the same. It was said "the mere harshness of the bargain was no defense." In 

Turner v. Baker (225 Pa. 359–1909), the court said: "If an owner of real estate chooses to make a contract with a broker in which it is stipulated that the broker shall have the exclusive right to sell the property within a specified time and that he shall be entitled to receive a certain commission if the sale be made within that time designated, no matter who makes it, he is bound by its terms and cannot be relieved from the bad bargain because his agreement may have been foolish or improvident.'

Form of Exclusive Contract. A form of exclusive contract which goes to the uttermost extent to favor the broker as against the owner is that which came before the Court in Werner v. Hindle 129 Pa. Super. Ct. 37-1937 and which reads as follows: 

"The undersigned being the owner, hereby employs A. C. 

Werner, with the sole and exclusive right for the sale of dwellings and ground, Lincoln Highway, Langhorne, Pa., for the term of three months, and in consideration of his undertaking to sell such property and ground, agrees to pay him a commission of 5% of the sale price upon its sale, transfer or exchange by whomsoever the same may be made or effected ***. The broker's exclusive authority here under may be revoked after expiration of the above term, by giving a written notice to the broker at least 30 days prior to the expiration of the current time, provided no negotiations are pending for the sale, transfer, or exchange of the same at that time, said 30 day notice to be the essence

$523 

of this agreement. But in default of such notice this exclusive contract shall renew itself from term to term as an exclusive contract with all its conditions remaining in force until notice herein provided shall be given to terminate. 

If subsequently the same shall be sold, transferred or exchanged to anyone with whom the broker has heretofore been negotiating, then the full commission shall be paid to the broker. 

Witness my hand and seal this 28th day of November 1934. 

Owner, William Hendel. (Seal)

This contract was held by the Superior Court to be unambiguous and enforceable.

Where an exclusive agency contract fixes a stipulated period and provides it shall become null and void upon the expiration thereof, provided however no negotiations are pending at such time of the sale of the property and after the expiration of that period, the owner permitted the broker to continue negotiations and to eventually conclude them successfully, the owner cannot be permitted to accept the work of the broker up to the date of sale and after the consummation refuse to pay commission. (Moran v. Bair 304 Pa. 471–1931).

Where a broker is merely appointed sole agent to sell real estate at a stipulated price, upon a stated commission, without a clause that the broker shall be entitled to receive his commission if the sale be made within the time designated no matter who makes it, and the broker does not produce a purchaser during the term of the contract, ready able and willing to purchase the property, he cannot recover his commission. 

195. When a Broker Is Not Entitled to His Commission. 

(a) Failure to Perform Conditions of his Employment. 

As we have seen in the preceding section, a broker is not entitled to commission unless he was expressly or impliedly employed. Nor can he claim commissions unless he completes his part of the contract of employment according to

§195 (b)  

its terms (Shapira v. Union Trust Company, Extr. 306 Pa. 

35, 39-1931). So where a broker was employed to sell land for a certain sum "net" to the owner, the broker is not entitled to a commission where the purchaser secured, does not pay more than the net price (Fink v. Dougherty 90 Pa. Super. 

Ct. 443—1927): nor where the broker's purchaser of a hotel business conditions his agreement to buy upon obtaining an extension of a lease from the owner of the building in which the business is located (Business Realty Company v. Schaffer 

99 Pa. Super. Ct. 175-1930): Nor is a broker entitled to his commissions where his purchaser was not willing to comply with the terms and conditions of sale without change or modification (Hagenlocher v. Walker, 113 Pa. Super. Ct. 4451934); nor where a broker's commissions are made conditional upon the actual procuring of a signature to a lease and a down payment thereon (Barber v. Miller 41 Pa. Super. Ct. 4421909).

Where the contract of employment was to pay a commission, if the broker procures a purchaser at the price $225. per foot and the purchaser offers only $150., the broker cannot recover, even though the purchaser subsequently buys the land from a person to whom the owner subsequently sold it (Elin v. 

Mark 288 Pa. 186-1927). In short, a broker is not entitled to his commission until he has completed the task according to his contract of employment (Garvin v. Reardon Bros. 95 

Pa. Super. Ct. 351-1928; Trainer v. Fort 310 Pa. 570—1933).

But as we have seen (§194, (b) 3, Supra.) where a broker has an exclusive contract for a definite period and is in negotiation with a prospective purchaser as the time expires, the owner cannot thereafter conclude the agreement directly with such purchaser and avoid liability for commission. (Walker v. Randal 85 Pa. Super. Ct. 443-1925). For a case on commissions earned by exchange of properties see (Reel v. Oravetz 279 Pa. 147-1924). 

(b) When the Employment is to Sell Property for a Fidu ciary. Where a broker is employed by a trustee or an executor he is bound to know that the law requires such a fiduciary

$525 

to dispose of trust property upon the most advantageous terms which it is possible for him to secure for the benefit of the estate, and his (the broker's) right to a commission for procuring a purchaser may be defeated by the fiduciary's duty to reject such purchaser if a better offer be received for the property before title has passed. So where a broker produced an admittedly responsible purchaser, who actually signed an agreement of sale in terms satisfactory to the trustees, he was held not entitled to his commission because the trustees before signing, received a better offer which they were in duty bound to accept. (Clark v. Provident Trust 

Company, Trustee et al., 329 Pa. 421-1938). (c) Disloyal brokers not entitled to Commission. A real estate broker must not be disloyal to his principal or he forfeits his right to commissions. His duty is one of utmost loyalty and he must make a full disclosure of what he knows which will enable his principal to act for his best interests. 

Thus a broker must not conceal from the owner the fact that he or his wife are the real purchaser (Shepard v. Kaufman, 88 

Pa. Super. Ct. 57-1926). Where a broker concealed from his principal the name as well as the fact that a purchaser was the owner of the adjoining lot so that the principal could not raise his price, the broker was held not to be entitled to his commission (Wilkinson v. McCullough 196 Pa. 205-1900). 

So too, where the broker secretly represents the buyer whom he knows will pay more than his offer (Central Turnverein v. 

Fitzpatrick, 238 Pa. 532-1913). If a broker knows the purchaser to be a straw-man, or even if he knows the facts and circumstances which upon investigation might lead to such a conclusion, and does not report these facts to his principal, he will lose his right to commission (Raisch v. Cook, 306 Pa. 

208-1932). In other words a real estate broker owes his principal the utmost good faith and cannot recover for his services if he has been guilty of any breach of that duty (id.) (d) Secretly Representing Both Buyer and Seller. 

An all too familiar example of disloyalty which bars the right to a commission is where a broker undertakes secretly

§196 

to represent both buyer and seller. The philosophy of the principle that "no man can serve two masters" extends as far back as the Holy Writ and is received as a cardinal principle in every system of enlightened jurisprudence" says the 

Supreme Court in Everhart v. Searle (71 Pa. 256-1872). 

This principle has been followed in many cases among which may be mentioned Linderman v. McKenna (20 Pa. Super. Ct. 

409-1902) where a broker employed to effect a sale of real estate at a fixed price, made a like contract with the prospective purchaser without informing his employer; was held not entitled to commissions from either. The same is true where he attempts to represent both vendor and vendee without disclosure of that fact to them. (Addison v. Wanamaker, 185 Pa. 536-1898; Central Turnverein etc. v. Fitzpatrick; 

238 Pa. 532-1918; Marshall v. Reed, 32 Pa. Super. Ct. 601906).

But when a real estate broker makes such a contract with full knowledge and consent of the parties, he may then recover his commission (Girts v. Shaw, 70 Pa. Super. Ct. 3451918); and subsequent ratification like prior permission, excuses this misconduct on the part of the broker and entitles him to his commission as against the one who condones the misconduct (Moore v. Grow, 1 Pa. Super. Ct. 125-1896). 

So also it has been held the parties affected by the conduct of the broker may with full knowledge of the circumstances, waive their right to refuse to pay commissions. (Custis v. 

Wilf, 300 Pa. 574-1930). 

196. Relation of Broker to Client Regarded by Law as Con fidential.

The relation between a real estate broker and his principal is a confidential one and therefore he must be loyal and steadfast in his allegiance (Everhart v. Searle, 71 Pa. 256-1872).

Somewhat akin to the situation where a broker represents both buyer and seller is that in which the broker himself is interested in the purchase. In such case, the confidential relationship forbids an agent employed to sell to make him-

$527 

self the purchaser; and if employed to purchase, he cannot himself become the seller. This rule will be rigidly enforced, unless it clearly appears that the parties for whose protection it was intended, have with full knowledge of all the circumstances agreed to waive their rights thereunder. (Finch v. 

Conrade's Excr. 154 Pa. 326-1893; Sloan v. Miller, 282 Pa. 

1-1924). Where a principal's land is sold by his broker to one of the broker's employees and it is afterwards resold at a profit, the broker may be compelled to account for all profits which the employer made out of the transaction, (Powers v. 

Black, 159 Pa. 153–1893).

Aside from the penalty inflicted by law upon a disloyal agent, it is to the best interest of the broker to make as advantageous a bargain for his client as possible, otherwise he will soon find himself discredited and his business ruined. An agent who deliberately buys from a client through a strawman and re-sells to a purchaser for a higher price is guilty of a gross fraud, the discovery of which will inevitably result in destroying his business reputation, while he will be compelled by court to return his ill-gotten gains and may besides lose his broker's license. The courts of equity are prompt to give relief when such a betrayal of confidence takes place no matter under what device concealed (Powers v. Black supra.). No broker ought therefore, ever purchase from his principal unless he does it openly and with full consent of his principal and not even then, if he does it merely for the purpose of reselling to a person whom he knows is desirous of purchasing. 

197. Authority of Real Estate Brokers to Act as Agents. 

The general rules of the law of agency apply to real estate brokers. In other words a broker has not only such authority as his principal expressly gives him, but also such as is apparently conferred upon him by the conduct of his principal. 

Thus where a real estate broker acts contrary to his instructions, his principal will nevertheless be bound by such acts as are within the scope of the authority which the agent was

§197  

held out to the world to possess (McNeile v. Cridland 168 Pa. 

16-1895; Grasseli Chemical Co. v. Biddle Co., 22 Pa. Super.

Ct. 426-1903). So too, an act done in excess of authority, if ratified either expressly or by conduct becomes just as binding upon the principal as though he previously authorized it. Thus, where an agent sells for a less price than that fixed by the owner, if the owner accepts the deposit money knowing of his broker's violation of instructions, he is held to have ratified the bargain.

The burden of proving the agency and the extent of the authority thereunder is on him who asserts it (Pore v. Duke 

303 Pa. 528-1931), and the authority of the agent cannot be established by the agent's own representation (Judith v. 

Dicola 317 Pa. 353-1935). Therefore, one dealing with an agent is bound to ascertain the extent of the agent's authority, which is prima facie limited to the powers expressly granted or arising by implication from the character of the grant 

(Culbertson Rec. v. Cook et al. 308 Pa. 557-1932; Lewis v. 

Matias 300 Pa. 238-1930). So letters of attorney are subjected to strict interpretation and the authority conferred is never extended beyond that which is given in express terms or which is necessary and proper to carry into effect that which is expressly given (Gross v. Kincaid, 83 Pa. Super. Ct. 514-1924).

It has been held in case of loss through embezzlement, where an agent negotiating a loan is agent for both lender and borrower, that the lender should suffer in preference to the borrower because he permitted the transaction to be conducted in such way as to give opportunity for the loss to occur (Philadelphia Trust Company v. Roberts, 14 W. N. C. 123-1883. 

Cavanaugh v. Buehler, 120 Pa. 441-1888).

The importance of ascertaining the limit of a broker's authority, is well illustrated by the following instances. The authority to loan money and take security for its payment, implies no authority to collect it (Isaacs v. Zugsmith, 103 Pa. 

77-1883). Conversely an agent authorized to collect and receive payment of interest on a mortgage, has no authority

§529 

real or apparent to receive payment of the principal (Shay v. 

Schrink, 335 Pa. 94-1939; Judith v. Dicolo, 317 Pa. 3531935). Neither does the bare authority to sell land give the agent a right to collect a mortgage given in part payment thereof (Lewis v. Matias, 300 Pa. 238-1930). Nor does the marital relation of itself constitute a husband the agent of his wife to receive payment of her mortgage (Shay v. Schrinck 

Supra.). And the authority to enter into a lease for the principal does not imply any authority in the agent to cancel or receive a surrender of it (Murphy v. Losch, 148 Pa. 1711892). Nor has an agent employed to collect rents, any authority to accept the surrender of the lessee's term (Jones v. Silverman, 95 Pa. Super. Ct. 336-1928; Edmundson v. 

Singer Sewing Machine Co., 51 Pa. Super. Ct. 545-1912). 

Even an attorney at law, retained to foreclose a mortgage, was held not to have authority to bind his client by a promise to pay taxes in consideration of a prior lien holder not bidding at the sheriff's sale (Starling v. West Erie Ave. B. & L. Asso. 

333 Pa. 124-1939).

The authority of an agent to sign an agreement of sale for the principal, must be in writing, else under the Statute of Frauds, it would not be binding (Parrish v. Koons, 1 Pars 78 -1844; Darlington v. Darlington, 160 Pa. 65-1894). Even a written contract of employment under seal of a broker as sole agent to sell a property does not confer upon such broker the authority to sign an agreement of sale on behalf of his principal (Gorsuch v. Berman, 270 Pa. 8—1921). Nor can a lease for a longer term than three years be executed by an agent unless his principal's authority to do so is in writing. 

If it is therefore desired to confer on a broker authority to execute an agreement of sale or a lease for a longer term than three years, good practice requires that the broker see to it that there is included in his written contract of employment, express authority to do so. 

198. Right of Principal to Cancel Agency. 

The principal, unless bound by an agreement for a definite period, may revoke the agency of the broker at any time

$199 

though it would seem he must give the broker a reasonable opportunity to perform (Shepler v. Scott 85 Pa. 329-1877), and he cannot take his property out of the broker's hands during the pendency of negotiations for the mere purpose of depriving him of his commission (Kelly v. Marshall, 172 Pa. 

396-1896 Walker v. Randal, 85 Pa. Super. Ct., 443—1925). 

Where the agent has partly performed his contract, he is of course entitled to some compensation when the authority is revoked (Stamets v. Deniston, 193 Pa. 548-1899). Nor can a broker be deprived of his commissions where within the time limit of his employment, he begins negotiations with a purchaser which are not concluded until after the agency has expired (Bossart v. Erie Coal Mining Co. 276 Pa. 63-1923).

A real estate broker has no lien for commissions or expenses on title papers placed in his possession by the client for the purpose of effecting a sale of the property. (Arthur v. Sylvester 105 Pa. 233–1884). Nor does a conveyancer have a lien for services except on the instruments drawn by him (id.). 

199. When a Broker Is Personally Liable. 

Every broker or agent impliedly agrees, when employed, that he will use reasonable care, skill and diligence in performing his duties. As said by Mr. Justice Coulter in Wingate v. 

Mechanics Bank (10 Pa. 104, 108-1848): "If an agent undertakes to do a specific thing for a stipulated reward, he is bound to exercise due diligence to accomplish what he has agreed to do, and to observe good faith towards his principal in every step, either of success or failure towards accomplishing the end. The law implies a promise from the broker, bankers, agents and attorneys, that they will severally in their respective callings, exercise competent skill and proper care in the service they undertake to perform, in which if they fail, an action lies to recover damages for breach of their implied promise."

By law, whenever a person presumes to act for another without authority, or if he exceeds his authority, he is per-

§ 

531 sonally liable to a person with whom he deals for his principal. 

So, where an agent bids at a sheriff's sale, a sum higher than he was authorized to bid, he becomes personally liable and not the principal (Hampton v. Speckenagle, 9 S. & R. 2121822; Simpson v. Kerkeslager, 41 Pa. Super Ct. 347-1909; 

Wolff v. Wilson, 28 Pa. Super Ct. 511-1905). And where a real estate agent having only oral authority to sell, executes an agreement of sale to the purchaser and the owner refuses to convey, the agent is personally responsible to the purchaser and the agent cannot recover the amount of a judgment against him from the owner (Krewson v. Fisher, 78 Pa. Super. Ct. 

509-1922). Should the principal, however, ratify the excess of authority, the principal becomes liable, and the personal liability of the agent disappears (Hopkins v. Everly 150 Pa. 

117-1892). While a broker in negotiating a sale is generally not liable, upon his principal's refusal to complete the contract to the purchaser, for a return of the deposit money paid, which the broker had turned over to his principal there is no principle of law which prohibits a broker from entering into a contract with a purchaser and assuming the duty to return the deposit money paid by the latter upon the failure of the principal to comply with the contract (Lucas v. Bode & Heinz, 94 Pa. Super. Ct. 248—1928).

Mr. Fallon, quotes three cases under which an agent may be held personally liable as follows: 

1. Where an agent makes a false representation of his authority with intent to deceive. 

2. Where with knowledge of his want of authority, but without intending fraud, assumes to act as though he were fully authorized. 

3. Where he undertakes to act, bona fide believing he has the authority but in fact has none, as in the case of an agent acting under a forged power of attorney. 

To which a fourth may be added as follows: 

4. If a broker acting for an undisclosed buyer signs an agreement of sale in his own name he may be held personally

§199  

liable Beymer v. Bonsall, 79 Pa. 298-1875, and if the principal is afterwards disclosed the other party to the contract may at his election, sue either the agent or the principal (Pittsburgh 

Terminal Coal Corp. v. Williams, 70 Fed. 2d (3 C. C. A.), 651934. Pittsburgh Terminal Coal Corp. v. Bennett 73 Fed. 2d 

387-1934).

In conclusion, it may be said, that highest fidelity to client, fair dealing with others, has its own reward, often material, which should make such conduct attractive aside from the penalties that are visited upon those guilty of breach of good faith.

§207-C. Liability of Broker as Conveyancer in Respect to Title Insurance 

561 

200. Title Insurance Companies. 

Not the least important part of a real estate transaction is the search or examination of the title and the settlement, or, as it is called in some states, the closing. This was the work of the conveyancer, but a remarkable change has come over the business of conveyancing, especially in the larger cities, due mainly to the modern tendency toward specialization in all business. In the real estate business it has resulted in the formation of companies making a specialty of searching and 533

$200 

insuring titles, called title insurance companies.¹ In Philadelphia, seventy-five years ago, a conveyancer, besides drawing the necessary papers, always made the title searches, the correctness of which he certified by signing the brief of title. 

There was, however, no guarantee other than the personal integrity and reputation of the man (Watson v. Muirhead, 57 

Pa. 161). But in those days this was no small thing. The conveyancers, inheritors of the tradition of the English solicitors, were a splendid set of men, proud of their calling and jealous of their unblemished reputations. They were well grounded in the law and their opinions on real estate law were often sought after as eagerly as a lawyer's. Indeed, the examinations necessary to gain admission to their conveyancers' associations were considered in those days to be more difficult than the lawyer's examination for admission to the bar. 2 The Centennial year of 1876 saw the incorporation of the first title insurance company in Pennsylvania, in fact in the world. This company was organized by several members of the Conveyancers' Association who saw the value of co-operation as well as specialization in the field of title searching. 

They foresaw that the time was coming when people would not be satisfied with the guarantee consisting solely of an individual's integrity, however spotless, but would demand some financial assurance. Accordingly, the new company issued a policy of title insurance backed by its capital, guaranteeing its examination of title. So successful was this first company that others followed until nowadays practically all of the searching in Philadelphia and much of it elsewhere is done by these companies.

The old time conveyancers have disappeared. Few modern conveyancers bother with searching titles, and the profession of conveyancing as once understood no longer exists. In cer- 

1 See an interesting address of Emil Rosenberger, president of the Real Estate

Title Insurance and Trust Co., made to Real Estate Brokers' Association of Philadelphia, March 2, 1911, published by Real Estate Title Insurance and Trust Co. 

2 The Real Estate Title, Insurance and Trust Co., Philadelphia, Pa., now merged and known as the Land Title Bank and Trust Company.

§535 

tain parts of the State, however, title searches are still made by the local lawyers. The old time conveyancers have been replaced by the modern real estate brokers, whose special business it is to buy, sell and lease real estate for others. He still draws agreements and prepares the instruments of conveyancing,' but leaves the field of title searching to the title insurance companies, who do it more expeditiously and perhaps better than he could. The modern real estate broker is not so well grounded in the law as the old time conveyancer, nor does he need to be, for questions of defect in title are no longer for him to decide, but are determined by the experts employed by the title companies. All in all, however, the real estate business has lost nothing by its abandonment of title searching. Rather have brokers been enabled to turn their energies in other directions of their vast field. Title insurance companies are here to stay, and it becomes necessary therefore in a modern book to dwell briefly upon the methods by which the title companies carry on business, and the way settlements are conducted. Where, however, the title insurance company acts as a conveyancer only in searching the title, it is liable as such (Bodine v. Wayne T. & T. Co., 33 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 68—1907). 

201. Application for Title Insurance. Rates. 

The application for title insurance is usually made by the attorney or agent of the vendee or mortgagor, for the vendee or mortgagor by custom in this State bears the costs of the examination fees. The application blanks are obtainable at any of the title companies and are similar to the one set out on page 536 the application is usually filled up by the com- 

1 The Acts of Apr. 28, 1899, P.L. 117, 1913 P.L. 80 and April 24, 1933 P.L. 66 

(17 P.8. 1608), Act of July 12, 1935, P.L. 708 (No. 271), 17 P.S. 1610, prohibit the practice of law except by a lawyer duly admitted to practice. A real estate broker, however, may prepare deeds and other appropriate instruments relating to property of which he or his associates have negotiated the sale or lease (Childs v. 

Smeltzer, 315 Pa. 9).
