If the applicant, his agent or attorney knows of any objections to the title, such as secret liens, easements, outstanding titles

§201 

or other objections, he is in duty bound to disclose them to the insuring company or he takes the risk of taking the title subject to such objection and absolving the company from any loss or liability by reason of the same. 

The cost of title insurance varies. The minimum charges of most companies for a property in the City of Philadelphia involving one chain of title only which has never been insured by such company is $50., which includes the minimum examination fee of $35. and the minimum premium of $10.00 for a $2000. policy of title insurance, together with a settlement fee for making the actual settlement or closing of title of $5.00. 

The premium in Philadelphia is 12 of 1% of the purchase price, or amount of the mortgage, where only mortgagee insurance is involved. The examination of the title to properties adjoining or in the same block and in the same title is made at a reduced rate; and where title to a property has been insured, a reduction is made for re-examination where a mortgage insurance is requested by the owner already insured. 

The present title charges on insurances of titles in counties other than Philadelphia vary considerably; the premium rates for mortgage titles being only 14 of 1% throughout the State, and for owner's insurance 1/2 of 1% everywhere excepting in 

Delaware, Montgomery, Chester and Bucks Counties, where it is only 14 of 1%. The title companies also issue sheriff distribution policies (for Philadelphia County), insuring the sheriff in distributing the proceeds of a sheriff's sale among the lien holders, mortgagees and owners, and also mandamus policies insuring the distribution of funds or monies arising from the taking of title to property by the municipality or State or for the damage to property for public purposes, as well as general and partial searches involving, in case of a search only, no examination of title. The charges for searches vary according to the period covered and the kind of search made, the liability of the company, however, on account of such a search is limited. A commission is paid by the title insurance company to the attorney or broker making the application for insurance.

§

539 

202. Settlement Certificate. 

The 

Upon receipt of the application the company makes the searches and prepares its brief of title and notes upon a paper called a settlement certificate, all of the encumbrances, liens and objections to the title. Two copies of the settlement certificate are sent to the applicant for title insurance, one copy being for the use of the seller or mortgagee in the transaction. Our Supreme Court has held that a notation upon a settlement certificate of an objection or encumbrance is not conclusive of its existence, i.e., it may be removed or explained by the vendor or owner and is merely tentative. (Riling v. 

Idell, 291 Pa. 472-1928). Ordinarily, the company has no liability until actual settlement is made (Petroleum Products 

Co. v. Guarantee T. & Tr. Co., 64 Pitts. L.J. 774—1916). 

form of settlement certificate appears on pp. 540 and 541. 

From this certificate the grantee may see at a glance what the defects, if any, are in the title as well as liens and encumbrances against it. These objections, as they are termed, may then be taken up by the grantee or his agent with the grantor and arrangements made for their removal. If not removed, these objections will appear on the policy to be issued by the company as exceptions which are not insured against. Take the form of settlement certificate on page 540, which was issued upon the application of an intending mortgagee, as an example. It discloses, first, that there are no ground rents against the property about to be mortgaged. 

There is, however, a mortgage existing against the property in the sum of $2,000 for three years at six per cent interest. 

The names of the mortgagor and mortgagee follow, together with the date, date of record and page of the mortgage book in which it is recorded. Next we see there are no registered taxes, i.e., taxes prior to the current year. But the current taxes are not paid nor is the current water rent. 

While no mechanics liens are cited as having been filed, notice is given that alterations are being made on the property which might possibly result in mechanics liens. No judgment liens exist.

Proof to be furnished showing the consideration for, and the purpose of the present transaction, and that same does not come within the 

Bankruptcy and Insolvency Laws.

Possibility of claims for repairs, additions or alterations or cleaning wells within six months. 

Any variation in location or dimensions and any other objections and easements which a survey for conveyance made by an official surveyor would disclose.

Attention is called to the regulations of the Bureau of Highways of March 13, 1924, forbidding the opening of street paving (or repaving) until five years from date of its completion. 

Additional Water Rent and any excess or other charges which may be imposed hereafter by the Water Bureau for current or prior years are excepted.

Subject to terms and conditions of any unrecorded lease.

Subject to Zoning Regulations of the City of 

Philadelphia and any violation of same.

All papers should be executed at the 

Office of the Company. 

In order to protect the insured from loss by reason of defects arising between this date and the final settlement it is recommended that the consideration money be deposited with the Company, and if the amount be sufficient the Company will then pay off the incumbrances. 

And when the transaction is settled and the papers recorded, which recording must be done by the Company, a Policy of Insurance for $2,000. in the Company's usual form will be issued in conformity with 

Application No. 444321. 

Thomas Jones

Title Officer.

§202 

Finally follow the printed stereotype objections, as well as others which are self-explanatory including building restrictions. Restrictions frequently prohibit the use of property for specified purposes and should be examined carefully by the intending mortgagee or buyer as they may have an adverse effect upon the mortgage or purchase; in fact they may prohibit the use of a property as a store, apartment house or the like, and may render a purchase valueless to the buyer. 

These restrictions are usually set out by the title company upon the back of the settlement certificate. 

With this settlement certificate before him, the broker can at a glance determine what serious defects or encumbrances are against the owner's title, and he sets out to either remove them or satisfy the mortgagee in some other way. The mortgage about to be insured is to be a first mortgage as a building association in Pennsylvania can now take only first mortgages, hence arrangements must be made to have the one disclosed by the certificate removed. The broker must ascertain if the mortgage is due or has been extended. If the mortgage is not yet due, a request must be made of the mortgagee to accept the principal at this time. Sometimes a premium for prepayment of the principal is asked by the mortgagee because he thinks he has a good investment and is reluctant to give it up. If, however, the new mortgage were to be a second mortgage, then nothing need be done, since the settlement certificate only shows one mortgage against the property.

The taxes and water rents can be paid at the settlement. 

Where there are registered taxes, someone should procure bills from the Office of the Receiver of Taxes, showing the amount due and the interest and penalty. Likewise, bills should be procured for taxes and water rents for the current year.

With regard to possible mechanics liens: If the alterations or work done are extensive and they have been completed and paid for, the contractor's release of liens should be demanded, or if a waiver of liens has been filed this should be verified.

$543 

1 The receipt or receipts showing payment for the work is also required by the title insurance company. If, however, the cost of the alterations or work is small, the title insurance company frequently will accept an affidavit by the owner that all bills have been paid in full. In any event the title insurance company requires an affidavit either that all bills have been paid in full or that no repairs or alterations have been made to the premises within the preceding six months. 

In cases where a new building has been erected upon the premises or very extensive alterations or repairs made within six months preceding the date of settlement, the title insurance company frequently requires a bond of indemnity from a responsible individual or surety company guaranteeing it against loss if it afterwards should appear that all bills have not been paid in full. For insurance against the possibility of unpaid bills for work or materials, the title insurance company usually requires the payment of an additional or special premium placing upon its policy an objection that unmarketability of title by reason of unfiled mechanics liens is expected but actual loss by reason of such unfiled mechanics liens is insured against by the policy (Wheeler v. Real Estate Title etc. Co. 160 Pa. 408-410-1894). The mechanics lien law of 

Pennsylvania (Act June 4, 1901, P.L. 431 49 P.S. 1 et seq.) gives to a mechanic or material man furnishing labor or material towards the erection of a new building or any extensive alterations to an old building a right to file a lien in court, if not paid, within six months from the beginning of the work and a right under certain restrictions when the repairs are not extensive (§10, Act 1901, P.L. 431 49 P.S. 52), within three months after the claimant's work is completed. The company excepts unmarketability because a court may hold that the title is not a marketable one, e.g. such as a purchaser need not accept because of the possibility that a lien may be filed for work or materials. 

The "notice" objections present no difficulty as they merely bring to the attention of the insured certain conditions, and in effect, the company insures subject to these 1 A form of affidavit to remove objections appearing on settlement certificates will be found at page 571.

§202 

objections although they do not appear upon the policy. The printed stereotype objections sometimes are important and should be examined by the broker. Title insurance companies are apt to mark some objections off as to mortgagee which would not be removed as to an owner because the risk involved in insuring a mortgagee is much less than that involved where an owner's insurance is issued. 

Settlements are generally made at the title company and the papers will be executed under the supervision of the title company, stamps duly attached and recorded by it. In Philadelphia at this time, in addition to Federal stamps, City stamps also are required to be affixed to certain papers. 

The old title papers are not often produced at the time of settlement, but their production in some measure insures the company that the party making the settlement is the party owning the property. The company generally satisfies itself as to the identity of the parties and that they are of full age. 

There have been cases where the owner of the property has been impersonated by another and the deed or mortgage by the impersonator has been insured (see Land Title & Tr. Co. v. Northwestern National Bank 196 Pa. 230—1900).

The company likewise satisfies itself that an adequate consideration is passing either for the mortgage or conveyance so that generally the transaction is not subject to be set aside in Bankruptcy or Insolvency proceedings. The objection as to repairs is removed by the affidavit hereinbefore referred to.

Unless an official survey is produced, the objection as to any variation in location or dimensions, etc., remains. Sometimes this objection is removed as to mortgagee by the insuring company because the mortgage liability is frequently ended by payment of the mortgage. The addition of the words in this objection "for conveyance" were necessitated by the decision of the Superior Court in Pa. Laundry Co. v. 

Land Title and Trust Co., 74 Pa. Super. Ct. 329-1920, wherein the insuring company claimed that, since an official survey was not produced, the use of a two feet six inches wide

$545 

alley gained by adverse user by an adjoining owner was covered in the title insurance policy by the objection as to accuracy of description although no specific objection was noted thereon. If a survey had been produced, this objection, the company contended, would have been noted thereon. 

Sometimes upon an examination of the premises after settlement, the city makes additional charges for water due to the additional use of water, for a period prior to the time when the settlement is made. The company excepts this but frequently will remove this objection as to a mortgagee. 

Of course, if the lease under which the tenant in possession occupies the premises antedates the mortgage insured or the conveyance to the new purchaser, the objection as to such lease would remain upon the settlement certificate. Sometimes, indeed, the lease may contain a valid option to buy, which may have an adverse effect on the transaction. 

The objection as to zoning regulations is placed upon all policies although it would seem that all owners should take subject to this objection by law. 

If a survey from an official city surveyor is furnished, it may disclose objections such as the fact that fences are not on the deed lines, or that buildings overlap adjoining properties. 

These objections, of course, would be added to the settlement certificate while the objection as to any variation in dimensions, etc., would be removed. All of the objections which are to be removed may be taken up in advance with the proper officer of the Title Company and are usually disposed of at the time of settlement as hereinafter set forth (see $207-3). 

At the time of settlement, the title company decides what objections are to remain and what objections are to be removed. After the settlement (see infra.) and recording of the title papers, the settlement certificate is used as a basis for the preparation of the title insurance policy (see infra.). 

203. Approval of Form of Instruments. 

The instruments of conveyancing, deed, mortgage or as the case may be should be submitted before execution to the title company for its approval as to form. If in proper form they

$204 

are returned marked O.K. as to form. If not, they are returned with the objections thereto noted on the outside. They are then to be corrected according to objections. They should then be kept until ready for settlement. A deed can be executed at settlement; a mortgage should, however, be executed and returned to the company, so that it may record the mortgage two days before settlement for reasons explained heretofore ($126). 

204. Payment of Title Charges and Conveyancing Expenses. 

In Pennsylvania the expense of title searching must be paid by the purchaser, for our recording system puts him on notice of all recorded liens and encumbrances (Woods v. 

Farmere, 7 Watts 382-1838; Lance v. Gorman, 136 Pa. 200, 209-1881). "It is not necessary to tender a whole chain of title when the vendor tenders the deed. It is the duty of the purchaser to examine the title for himself. The purchaser is not bound to accept a doubtful title, but it is his business to show that it is doubtful or positively bad" (Coulter, J., in 

Espy v. Anderson, 14 Pa. 308-312-1850). In some jurisdictions, e.g., England, the vendor must furnish the purchaser with an abstract of title, but this is not the law in Pennsylvania (Espy v. Anderson, supra; Whittaker v. Williams, 7 Leg. & 

Ins. Rep. 14-1865; Negley v. Lindsay, 67 Pa. 217-1870. If the purchaser here wants an abstract of title he must make it himself or pay some one out of his own pocket to prepare it for him. But after the defects are disclosed, or any deeds, releases, etc., are found to be missing or unrecorded it is incumbent upon the vendor at his own expense to supply the lost or broken links in the chain of title (Frowert's Estate, 12 

Phila. 148-1878; Negley v. Lindsay, supra.).

As to the payment of conveyancing costs or charges for preparing and drawing the instruments, the practice is not uniform throughout the State and depends on local custom. 

In Philadelphia the burden of paying these charges is also on the purchaser (Frowert's Estate, 12 Phila. 148-1878; 

Callaghan v. McCredy, 48 Pa. 463-464-1865). In many other counties, the expense of preparing the deeds, etc., is on the Th

$547 

vendor, a custom arising probably from the fact that if the purchaser fails to consummate his purchase it is the duty of the vendor to tender an executed deed before bringing suit 

(Kester v. Rockel, 2 W. & S. 365-369-1841. But see Tiernan v. Roland, 15 Pa. 429, 440-1850; Eberz v. Heisler, 12 Pa. Super. Ct. 388-1900).

In the case of mortgages and assignments thereof, the rule is uniform that the borrower, or assignor of the mortgage must pay all charges, whether for title searching or conveyancing expenses, unless an express agreement is made to the contrary. The charge for making and acknowledging sheriff's deeds is to be paid by the purchaser (Act of July 11, 1901, P.L. 663, §1, 16 P.S. 2661). 

205. Settlements. (Conveyance). 

You are now ready for settlement. A settlement need not necessarily be made at the company's offices, though it is better to do so and in Philadelphia usually is. Fix a time for settlement convenient to the parties and make an appointment at the title company for that hour. The settlement is conducted by an employee of that company, known as settlement clerk, and is stated according to the form here shown. 

Suppose the facts of the transaction are as follows: 

William Simpson sold his property, 322 Logan Street, to

Louis Brown, for a total consideration of $3,000, the premises being subject to a mortgage of $1,500. Brown has paid $100. down on account. The mortgage interest at 6% is due semiannually on the first days of June and December. Taxes of 

1939, which are $81.75, appear from the settlement certificate as not paid. The water rent for 1939 has been paid and amounts to $16.00. The purchaser has borrowed from the 

H. B. Building and Loan Association $2,000. on a first mortgage. There is a perpetual fire insurance policy of $3,000., which the vendor agrees to sell at its cancellation value. The property is occupied by a tenant at a rental of $30. per month. 

The settlement is stated as of August 1, 1939. The following form of settlement sheet shows how this settlement would be stated:

$205

Louis Brown

Address 120 E. Manheim St. 

Grantee.

This settlement statement it will be seen, is divided into two parts, the first part under Dr. represents everything charged against the purchaser, i.e., everything he must pay. 

The other part, under the letters Cr., represents everything credited to him or for which he receives an allowance. Taking up the entries in order, we find the first item is the consideration. Where property is sold subject to a mortgage, the usual method of stating the consideration is to deduct the amount of mortgage and to state the net consideration. This is the same as debiting the purchaser with the gross consideration of $3,000. under the Dr. and crediting him with the $1,500. mortgage, to which he is to take subject under the 

Cr. Where the sale is clear as here, the full consideration is stated.

The next item is taxes. Turning to our settlement certificate we would find that the taxes for 1939 have not been paid by the grantor, and all of them cannot be charged against the purchaser or grantee. We charge the seller with the whole of the 1939 and penalties (see under Settlement with Grantor) and charge the purchaser under "Dr." with five months' taxes and water rents as the purchaser will be in possession for five months and have no taxes or water rents to pay to the city. 

As to the next item, the water rent, the situation is different. 

This has been paid for the year of 1939 by the grantor we will assume, but since he surrenders possession to the purchaser on the first of August he has had only seven months' use of the water, for which he has paid twelve months' rent. 

He ought, therefore, be reimbursed by the purchaser for five

§205  

months' water rent. Therefore, the five months' water rent or five-twelfths of $16.00 equals $6.67 which must be paid by the purchaser and is charged against him. House rent, if any by custom in Philadelphia, is usually also adjusted upon the same principles. This leaves acknowledgment of deed, which the purchaser pays and it is, therefore, charged against him.

The next item is fire insurance policy. The policy here is a perpetual one of $3000., the premium $60.00 and the cancellation value 10%, i.e., $6.00 less, or $54.00 (See §45(d), also as to term policies). Since the purchaser must pay this cancellation value in order to get the fire policy, it is charged against him under Dr. The purchaser thus pays a total of $3095.47.

Below "Cr." appears all items for which the purchaser or grantee receives credit. Having already paid $100.00 on account of the purchase price, the grantee is, of course, to be credited with this amount. Since the taxes and water rents have been adjusted above, no adjustment need be made under the item on the Cr. side. The rule, therefore, to be remembered with regard to taxes and water rents is that where the water rent or taxes for the current year have been paid by the grantor before settlement or are paid by him at settlement, the grantee must be charged for the proportionate value of the balance of the year. Where, however, they are not paid before or at settlement by the grantor, the grantee is credited with the proportionate value from the beginning of the year to the settlement. In this settlement, the water rent was paid before settlement, hence the grantee was charged with five months, the balance of the year; the taxes were paid at settlement, hence we likewise charge the grantee with five months' taxes and charge the grantor with the whole year's taxes in our settlement with him. 

If the property were being sold subject to a mortgage, we would have an item concerning mortgage interest in the settlement. Mortgage interest is usually payable half-yearly but not in advance. Hence, in such a case the grantee usually

$551 

receives credit for the proportionate amount of interest due from the last interest paying period to the date of settlement; the grantee when the next interest paying period arrives paying the full six months' interest, and thus in reality paying only interest from the time he receives title to the property to the date for the payment of the installment of interest. 

The tenant has paid rent at the rate of $30. per month to 

August 15th in advance. Credit the grantee with fifteen days' rent for the last half of the month, this rent being in the grantor's possession.

This ends the statement of settlement of accounts between the grantor and grantee, as far as their relations with each other are concerned. By adding up the debits and deducting total of credits we find that the grantor is entitled to receive from the grantee a total of $2980.47. 

The title company now states a settlement with the grantor and grantee separately. The settlement with the grantor at the lower left hand corner is simple. The first item is fund due grantor. This item is carried over from the balance due grantor as found above, to wit $2980.47. From this is deducted anything chargeable against the grantor alone. The principal of the mortgage of $1500. and interest to date and cost of satisfaction of the mortgage are first deducted. A commission for the sale to Brown is due Robert Smith, the real estate broker, of $150., and the United States Revenue stamps (at the rate of $1.00 per thousand of consideration) and Philadelphia City stamps are affixed to the deed and paid for by the grantor. Taxes for 1939 are deducted from the fund due the seller as the purchaser has paid his proportion from the date of settlement to December 31st above. The grantor pays for the acknowledgment of the deed, having received a credit for this from the grantee above and he likewise pays for an affidavit to clear certain objections from the certificate. The total of these items are deducted, leaving a net balance due the grantor of $1225.34, which amount the title company pays over to the grantor. 

The settlement with the grantee in this case is a little more

§206  

complicated. We start with the same sum, to wit, $2980.47, which the grantee must pay the grantor. In addition to this the grantee must pay the fee of the solicitor of the building association for preparing the bond and mortgage and attending the settlement of $25.00; the fee of Robert Smith for preparing the deed and attending to the settlement and other details, say $35.00; back dues to the building association of $20.00, being the money sufficient to take the purchaser back to the last series two months before for ten shares; revenue stamps of the City of Philadelphia on the bond accompanying the mortgage at the rate of 5¢ per hundred dollars; the title insurance charges including examination of title, premium and settlement fee of $60.; charges for recording deed to Mr. Brown and mortgage from Mr. Brown to the Building Association $11.50, and the fee for acknowledgment of the mortgage 50¢. 

The total sum due to the title company by the purchaser to complete the settlement is $3061.47. Of this amount, the building association advanced $2000. upon a first mortgage and the purchaser the balance of $1061.47. The settlement sheet is then approved by the buyer and seller or their agent or counsel and the title insurance company pays the various sums to the various parties as shown by the settlement sheet from the monies deposited with it. The recording of the deed and mortgage closes the transaction. It should be noted that frequently there is no back stock to be paid to a building association because building association mortgages are more frequently now direct reduction mortgages. 

206. Mortgage Settlement. 

The statement of a mortgage settlement is much simpler than the others. Suppose Owen Johnson, the owner of premises 314 X Street, desires to borrow $3200. on a first mortgage from the H. B. Building and Loan Association (a building association can now only take a first mortgage as security see $94(d)); and that the solicitor of that building association has made application for title insurance and received the settlement certificate set out on page 536; the settlement would be stated as appears in form, following.

$206 A

This settlement is self-explanatory and easily understood. 

The fund is deposited with the company by the mortgagee and the balance, after deducting expenses, charges and the principal and interest due upon an existing mortgage, taxes and water rents, is paid over to the mortgagor. 1 Frequently the mortgagee's attorney sends a letter of instructions to the title insurance company, setting forth the objections which may appear upon the mortgagee's policy together with a check. This, of course, places the burden of making a proper settlement including certification as to the identity of the parties upon the title insurance company. 

However, often it is advisable for the agent or attorney for the mortgagee to appear at the settlement for the purpose of checking facts including the consideration paid for the premises by the proposed mortgagor or other statements made by the prospective owner in the application for mortgage. 

206-A. Sheriff Settlements. 

Mention might be made of settlements with the sheriff after a sheriff's sale. Where property is bid in by a purchaser, 10% of the purchase price must be paid in cash at the time of the sale, or a minimum of $60. The bid at the sale must include, under the present regulations of the sheriff's office, all unpaid taxes, water rents, interests, penalties and municipal claims. Settlement should be made within twenty-one days after the sale at the sheriff's office. Here the balance of the purchase price is paid and disbursed by the sheriff. First, of course, he deducts taxes and water rents, municipal claims, taxes due the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, and then distributes the balance to the encumbrancers whose liens are discharged by the sheriff's sale in the order of their priority. 

Generally the creditor at whose suit the property was exposed to sale, whether a mortgagee, ground rent owner, or judgment creditor, is first paid after the payment of taxes, water rents and municipal claims as he is first in the order of priority. If the buyer at the sale is the same person as the plaintiff in the execution upon which the sheriff's sale is had, 1 For form of letter of instructions see $307 p. 788 of this book.

$555 

the sheriff under the Act of April 20, 1846, P.L. 411 (12 P.S. 

2511), permits the purchaser to deposit with the sheriff only the sum necessary to pay taxes, water rents and costs of the sale, a credit being noted for the balance of the purchase on the plaintiff's judgment upon which the sale is had. If the sale is on a mortgage or bond, the credit is noted on the same by the sheriff at the settlement. Any other purchaser, of course, must pay in cash to the sheriff at the time of the settlement the balance of the purchase money due. Generally the sheriff insists upon having a distribution policy from a title insurance company insuring him in the proper distribution of the fund, if a fund is realized from the sheriff's sale. 

207. Suggestions in Making Settlements. 

(1) In ordering title insurance take the old deed of the property about to be conveyed with you to the application clerk in order that he may get the description accurately. (2) If a mortgage is to be given by the purchaser at the same time that he gets title, order the title insurance in the name of the mortgagee. The policy will issue to him and the company will issue to the owner an owner's certificate which will give the owner a right to the new policy in his own name upon surrender of the mortgagee's policy when the mortgage is paid off and the right to sue with the mortgagee for a defect of title. (3) Before you fix a time for settlement make a list of the objections appearing on the settlement certificate which you insist on having removed and send to the vendor a duplicate copy of the settlement certificate which the company will furnish. Communicate with the grantor or his representative and ascertain his ability to have them removed. (See Groskin v. Knight Jr. 290 Pa. 274-1927 holding seller must be given this opportunity.) (4) In case the building has just been completed be sure that all work has been done by the builder satisfactory to the purchaser before settlement, or some security or money left

$207 

as security that the builder will complete the building satisfactorily. (5) In case awnings, shades or other personal property is included in the sale, it is wise to have a separate bill of sale for these articles. (6) If the property transferred is a factory or the like, make sure that machinery, fixtures and merchandise included in the sale are properly described in the deed and the accompanying bill of sale. (7) Always have the grantor bring to the settlement his current tax and water rent receipts, if they have been paid, in order that they may be removed from the settlement certificate should they appear as unpaid thereon. If any taxes or water rents are unpaid, obtain bills for the same including penalties and interest if any to the date of settlement. (8) Where the grantee takes subject to a mortgage see that the grantor produces the interest receipt of the last interest payment. (9) Always have grantee bring either cash or a certified check in order that there be no delay in paying out the funds. (10) If representing a mortgagee or grantee and you cannot attend settlement deposit the money with the title company, together with a letter of instruction' setting forth in detail what objections you insist on having removed and such other conditions as you desire to impose. The parties to the transaction, especially the grantor, ought always be present as well as properly identified so that possible imposition may be avoided. (11) See that the deed or other instrument is properly executed and dated. (12) Outside of Philadelphia, where the local custom is generally otherwise and unless the agreement of sale provides otherwise, taxes levied prior to the sale are not apportioned, 1 For form of such letter see $307, p. 788 of this book.

$557 

but are an encumbrance which the grantor is obliged to remove (See $50, Suggestion 3). Rent likewise paid in advance belongs to the grantor before settlement, except in Philadelphia by custom and except where the agreement of sale provides that it should be apportioned to the day of settlement ($50, Suggestion 3). (13) If the fire insurance policy is to be assigned, see that it is sent to the insurance company immediately after settlement, so that the transfer to the new owner may be approved by the company without delay. If it is to be assigned to a mortgagee don't forget to order a mortgagee clause to be attached. (14) If the house is occupied by the owner, see that the keys are turned over at settlement. Never leave the grantor in possession even for a short time unless he signs a lease for the property. If not occupied by the owner, see that the lease of tenant is assigned. Demand either a lease of the tenant or possession, for a tenant without a lease is a prolific source of trouble and litigation. (15) Where a mortgage encumbrance is to be paid off at settlement be sure to notify the mortgagee to send or have at the settlement an itemized statement of the principal and accrued interest which he demands to satisfy the mortgage. 

This will save much delay and a possible postponement of the settlement. (16) In arranging for a mortgage settlement where only a mortgage is made and title is not to pass, have the mortgage properly executed and in the possession of the title company at least two days before settlement, so that it may be recorded and the search brought down to the day of settlement, to disclose any liens that may have crept in just before or at the time the mortgage is recorded (See §126).

If settlement is not made through a title company, the conveyancer should take the same precaution. When, however, the mortgage is made at the same time that title passes this need not be done.

§207 A  

(17) When taking title to property from a guardian, trustee, administrator or executor selling by direction of the Orphans' Court, see that the title company has examined and approved the petition and decree and that security has been properly entered in accordance with the terms of the decree. 

207-A. The Title Insurance Policy. Nature of Remedies and Liabilities Thereunder. 

(a) Nature of a Title Insurance Policy. A Title Insurance 

Policy may insure a mortgagee, an owner, an assignee of mortgagee or assignee of a ground rent as the application for title insurance requests. The policy does not protect against defects, liens or encumbrances arising after the policy is issued 

(Foehrenbach v. German American Title Company, 217 Pa. 

333, 337-1907). Nor as to liens or defects which are expressly excepted in a schedule of exceptions set forth in the policy. One of the stereotyped exceptions reads, “accuracy of description and any other objections an official survey would disclose". In Pennsylvania Laundry Company v. Land Title, etc. & Trust Company, (74 Pa. Super. Ct. 329-1920), the property was subject to an easement of passage, which had been acquired by adverse user. Whether an easement of that nature would have been disclosed by an official survey was held to be a question of fact for the jury. 

The provisions of a title policy do not constitute the company a surety for the payment of a mortgage debt or for the consideration paid for the real estate, nor does it guarantee the payment of the same, or insure that the mortgaged premises are sufficient security for the debt. What the title company policy does is to indemnify against loss or damage by reason of defects of title and liens upon the land. (Whiteman v. Merion T. & T. Co., 25 Pa. Super. Ct. 320, 322—1904.)

Where the company insures against actual loss by reason of unfiled mechanic's liens (excepting unmarketability of title by reason of the possibility of such liens), as is frequently done where mortgages are placed upon a new building operation, separate policies are frequently issued, one insuring the 

---

§207A (b) 

559 

title and the other insuring against possibility of unfiled liens. 

Sometimes separate completion bonds are issued by surety companies insuring a mortgagee not only against unfiled liens but that the buildings contemplated will be fully completed in accordance with the agreed plans and specifications free and clear of liens. In cases where buildings are not completed, the courts have held that the surety company may be liable for the cost of completing the building if the contract is so drawn. (Mechanic's Trust Company v. Fidelity and Casualty 

Company, 304 Pa. 526.) Generally, however, under the form of title insurance policies usually prevailing, title companies insuring completion are only liable for actual loss by reason of non-completion of buildings. (Wheeler v. Equitable Trust 

Company, 206 Pa. 428-1903). 

(b) Nature of Liability Under the Policy. A Title Insur ance Company's liability may arise in one of three ways: (1) 

From negligence in making searches; (2) In conduct of settlement; (3) Under the policy is issued. These liabilities will briefly be considered separately. 

1. Liability for Negligent Searches. Where a Title Insur ance Company merely makes searches and issues what is called a "guaranteed search", its liability is merely that of a conveyancer who is liable only for negligence. (Petroleum Prod ucts Co. v. Guarantee T. & T. Co., 26 D.R. 297-1916). Where a Title Company issues a title policy and also acts as conveyancer, its liability for negligence as conveyancer is not merged into the title policy. (Bodine v. Wayne Title & T. Co., 33 Pa. Super. Ct. 68–1907.) 

2. Negligence in Conduct of Settlement. Where the Title 

Insurance Company makes settlement at its offices as hereinbefore stated, it charges the fee of $5.00 therefor. When settlement is thus made, the Title Company assumes in relation thereto the duties of a conveyancer and if it makes an error in the course of the settlement, which is not approved by the parties, it is liable to the party injured. (Pearson v. 

The Central National Bank, 102 Pa. Super. Ct. 111-1930.)

§207B  3. Liability Under the Policy Issued. A Title Company's liability under the policy issued by it, is that of an insurer and is generally held subject to the well-known rule of insurance law that a policy is to be construed most liberally in favor of the insured. (Hess v. Merion Title & T. Co., 317 Pa. 5011935.)

If at the time a title policy is delivered, the title is defective by reason of a lien, encumbrance or otherwise, the contract is breached and the company is immediately liable to the in sured. (Fifth Mutual Bldg. Society of Manayunk's Appeal, 

317 Pa. 161-1935). The loss suffered, however, is not to be ascertained as of the time the breach occurred but as of the time the demand is made. Thus, if at the time the demand is made for reimbursement, the mortgagor has not title and the insured second mortgage has no equity at the time over and above, the first mortgage, due entirely to shrinkage of value, there can be no recovery against the Title Company because the Title Company is not a surety for the mortgage debt nor a guarantor of its payment, and its policy merely indemnifies against loss or damage sustained by reason of defects of title and liens upon land. (Narberth Building and 

Loan Association v. Bryn Mawr Trust Company, 126 Pa. 

Super. Ct. 74-1937.) This, it was pointed out, follows because by the terms of the Title Insurance Policy the company has the right to take the estate or interest insured at its then market value, and in case of a disagreement as to value, the same must be fixed by three persons chosen in accordance with the terms of the policy.¹ 

207-B. Assignment of Title Policy. 

Title Insurance Policies cannot be assigned from a prior owner to a new owner, but the insurer will approve an assignment by a mortgagee to an assignee of a mortgagee or by the owner of a ground rent to the assignee of such ground rent. 

1 For further discussion of cases relating to measure of damages in suits v. 

Title Companies, see excellent article of Robert Brigham, Esq., "Notes on Title Insurance, Pennsylvania Bar Quarterly Oct. 1940 (vol. XII, page 20).

§207C 

561 

Where a mortgage is insured the company usually issues, upon the payment of a nominal charge, a paper called an owner's certificate, agreeing upon satisfaction of the mortgage insured, to issue a new policy in favor of the owner who executed the mortgage as of the date of the original mortgagee's policy. Where the mortgage was unpaid, formerly no recovery could be had against the Title Company upon the owner's certificate by reason of defects, of title until the mortgage had been satisfied. (Cherry v. People's Trust Company, 282 Pa. 52-1925.) Now, however, in case of a defect or encumbrance in the title, a recovery can presently be had thereon jointly by the owner of the mortgagee's policy and of the owner's certificate. 

207-C. Liability of Broker as Conveyancer in Respect to Title Insurance. 

A broker who represents his client at a settlement should not conclude that he is free from all liability where the title is insured for he may be liable for some act or omission, as where he neglects to inform his client of unpaid taxes which the Title Company has noted and which has not been removed. (Schwab v. Cornell, 306 Pa. 536.) Since the subject is sometimes not only a practical one but also a technical one, great care should be exercised in making of settlement pursuant to sale.

It is further agreed that this agreement shall apply to and bind the heirs, executors, administrators and assigns of the respective parties. 

IN WITNESS WHEREOF, the said parties to these presents have hereunto set their hands and seals the day and year first above written. 

Sealed and delivered in the presence of us 

JOHN H. ROYER, JACOB REYNOLDS. 

ISAAC SIMPSON.

ASSIGNMENTS 225. Assignment or Extinguishment of Ground Rent. 

See Form, $116, page 304. Note the same form can be used for both assignment and extinguishment of ground rents. If the instrument is made to a third person it operates merely as an assignment. If made to the holder of the fee it operates to extinguish it (see §§116-117 on merger).

FORMS.

JOHN DOE,

ROBERT ROE. 

ALLEN SMITH. (Seal.) 

This assignment is usually indorsed directly upon the lease assigned.

The agent's authority hereunder may be revoked by the owner at any time after the expiration of the above term, when no negotiations are pending for the sale and exchange of the property, but only upon and after thirty days' notice in writing to that effect given to the agent. And if, subsequently to such revocation, the property shall be sold or exchanged to any one with whom the agent had therefore been negotiating, the said commission will be paid to the said agent. DEEDS 231. Deed in Fee by Individual (Usual Form). 

See form, $53, page 116. 

232. Deed in Fee. (Short Form Under Act of 1909). 

See $57, page 152. 

233. Form of Deed by Corporation. 

See Deed form $53, page 116. Deeds both by and to Corporations are similar in form to the ordinary deed form of individuals except that after the name of the corporation is put the words "Its successors and assigns" instead of "heirs' as in the case of individuals. Also where conveyance is made 

---

$583 

FORMS. 

by a corporation take care to add the Corporation form of acknowledgment, as set forth in form §211. 

234. Deed to Husband and Wife as Tenants by Entirety. 

The deed form used to convey property to husband and wife as Tenants by Entirety is the same as the ordinary deed form shown in §53 on page 116, except that after the operative words or granting clause (see $54, (d)) and immediately before the description are inserted the words "As Tenants by Entireties" so that the clause would read as follows: "Do grant, bargain and sell, release and confirm unto the said Grantee their heirs and assigns As Tenants by Entireties, All That Certain, &c,” In the habendum, that is the To Have and To Hold clause, (See $55 a) insert the words, "As Tenants by Entireties" immediately after the habendum so that the habendum will read: 

"To have and To Hold said lot or piece of ground above described with the buildings and improvements thereon erected, hereditaments and premises hereby granted or mentioned and intended so to be with the appurtenances unto the said Grantees, their heirs and assigns to and for the only proper use and behoof of the said Grantees their heirs and assigns forever As Tenants by Entireties."

Court, that the lands hereafter described should be sold ac- 1 Until bond has been entered by the administrator or executor, selling under direction of the orphans' court, his deed is ineffectual to pass title, hence at the settlement demand the production of a certificate from the clerk of the orphans' court that bond has been entered in conformity to the decree of the court. It is good practice to record this certificate with the deed, though often this is not done. The best practice, however, is to have the clerk of the orphans' court certify on the deed both to decree authorizing the sale and to the entry of the bond. 

See end of deed, form $239, page 598. In this way all of the advantages of recording this certificate are obtained, while the cost of recording it as a separate instrument is saved.

238. Deed by Guardian ¹ for Lands Sold by Order of Orphans' 

Court. 

THIS INDENTURE, made the one thousand nine hundred and

A. D. 

day of ... ......9 between Patrick Coe, 1 Guardians are of two kinds, testamentary or such as are appointed by a deceased parent in his will; and appointive, or such as are appointed by orphans' court where a minor inherits property and no guardian has ever been appointed. 

In drawing a deed from a guardian first ascertain how the guardian was appointed. 

If by will, recite that part of the will. If by the orphans' court, recite that fact as above.

The portion in brackets is a covenant which is not essential to the validity of the deed and is often omitted. Until bond has been entered by the guardian his deed is ineffectual to pass title. Therefore, at settlement, a certificate of the clerk of the orphans' court must be produced, showing that bond has been entered in conformity with the order of the court. It is good practice to record this certificate with the deed though the more usual practice is to have the clerk of the orphans' court certify to the entering of the bond as shown at end of deed, form, $239, page 598. See also note to form, $237, page 588.

SMITH, his wife, EMMA E. STEARLY, of the said city, single woman, JOHN NEWTON, of the said city, and IDA I., his wife, and MARY S. RAINIER, of the said city, single woman, all of the first part; "THE FIDELITY INSURANCE TRUST AND SAFE DEPOSIT COMPANY, GUARDIAN, of the estate of 

Mary Leath Courtney, a minor, of the second part; and PETER 

S. DILDINE, of the city of Philadelphia, gentleman, of the other part.

WHEREAS, Jacob Stearly was, in his lifetime, lawfully seised in his demesne, as of fee, of and in the several lots or pieces of ground and premises hereinafter particularly described, with the appurtenances, and being so thereof seised as aforesaid, departed this life on the twenty-second day of July, Anno Domini one thousand eight hundred and eighty-three (1883) having first made and published his last will and testament in writing bearing date the fourteenth day of March, Anno

Domini, 1882, duly proven the thirteenth day of July, Anno Domini 1883, and remaining on record in the office of the register of wills in and for the City and County of Phila delphia, WHEREIN and WHEREBY, after directing the payment of his just debts and funeral expenses he, the said Jacob Stearly, did will as follows, viz.: "ITEM I give, devise and bequeath all the rest, residue and remainder of my estate, real, personal and mixed, whatsoever and wheresoever the same may be situate and of which I may die seised, possessed or entitled to in manner following, to wit: One full, equal fifth part thereof unto my grandchild, CLARA D. SMITH, her heirs, executors, administrators and assigns forever absolutely. One full, equal fifth part thereof unto my grandchild Emma E. Stearly,

$240 

her heirs, executors, administrators and assigns forever absolutely. One full, equal fifth part thereof unto my grandchild

Ida I. Newton, her heirs, executors, administrators, assigns forever absolutely. One full, equal fifth part thereof unto my grandchild Mary Rainier, her heirs, executors, administrators and assigns forever absolutely. And the remaining one-fifth part thereof unto my great-grandchild, Mary Leath Courtney, her heirs, executors, administrators, and assigns forever absolutely."

AND WHEREAS, All of the devisees named in said recited will are of full age except the said Mary Leath Courtney. 

AND WHEREAS, At an orphans' court for the City and County of Philadelphia, held at Philadelphia, on the twenty-ninth day of December, Anno Domini 1883, upon the petition of the said Mary Leath Courtney, who was then above the age of fourteen years, the said "The Fidelity Insurance Trust and Safe Deposit Company," was appointed guardian of her estate. AND 

WHEREAS, At an orphans' court for the city and county aforesaid, held at Philadelphia on the twenty-ninth day of November, Anno Domini 1884, the petition of the said "The Fidelity 

Insurance Trust and Safe Deposit Company," guardian as aforesaid, was presented setting forth "That the said Mary

Leath Courtney is seised in her demesne as of fee and in an equal undivided fifth part or share of and in the real estate hereinafter particularly described. That all the other parties interested in said real estate, being sui juris, are anxious and desirous of disposing of the same." That Peter S. Dildine, of the City of Philadelphia, has offered to purchase the said real estate for the price or sum of sixteen thousand dollars clear of all encumbrances, and all the other owners of said real estate have agreed to sell at said price, and are anxious and desirous, that the proper and necessary deeds and assurances for said real estate should be executed to the said Peter S. 

Dildine, and a perfect title for the same made to him, but are prevented from so doing on account of the inability of the petitioners to join with them in the execution and delivery of said deeds as guardian aforesaid, without the consent and

$601 

FORMS. 

order of the said court. That the said price offered for said real estate is much greater than the assessed value of same, and that the said amount is to the best of petitioner's knowledge and belief as large a price as can now be obtained for said property. That it is to the advantage of said minor's estate that the said real estate should be sold, inasmuch as the same is in great need of repair and in a dilapidated condition. The petitioners therefore prayed the court to approve of the price offered for said real estate and authorize them to sell the said minor's undivided fifth interest therein for onefifth of the said price, offered as aforesaid, viz.: $3,200; and that they be authorized, ordered and directed as guardian of said Mary Leath Courtney, to join with the other owners of the above mentioned real estate in the execution and delivery of the proper and necessary deed or deeds and other assurances needful and necessary for vesting and conveying said real estate to the said Peter S. Dildine, in fee simple. WHEREUPON the said court upon due consideration of the said petition, ordered and decreed that the said "The Fidelity Insurance Trust and Safe Deposit Co.," Guardian of said Mary Leath Courtney, be authorized to sell the said minors one full, equal and undivided fifth part or share of and in the real estate hereinafter particularly described for the price or sum of three thousand two hundred dollars to Peter S. Dildine, his heirs and assigns, and that the said "The Fidelity Insurance Trust and Safe Deposit Co.," guardian as aforesaid, be authorized and directed to make, execute and deliver, or join with the other owners of said real estate in the execution and delivery of, all and every deed or deeds or other assurances in the law necessary to vest said minor's undivided fifth part or share in said real estate in the said Peter S. Dildine, in fee simple, and that said minor's undivided interest in said real estate so sold be and remain to the said Peter S. Dildine, his heirs and assigns, firm and stable forever, and the said guardian was thereby authorized to receive and receipt for said purchase money. And the said court further ordered that security be entered by said guardian in the sum of six thousand four

$240 

hundred dollars, and approved of the bond of the said "The 

Fidelity Insurance Trust and Safe Deposit Company," as such security. Which security has since been duly entered.

244. Master's Deed in Partition in Equity. 

Whereas, On the 21st day of January, A. D. 1939, in the 

Court of Common Pleas No. 1, of Philadelphia county, in the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, sitting in equity of Dec.

Term, 1938, to number 921, a bill of complaint was filed by 

Maria Bloom, plaintiff, against Otto Bloom and Anna Bloom, defendants, setting forth (inter alia) that the parties therein named as plaintiff and defendants, together and undivided did hold a certain tract of land situate in said county, and bounded and described as follows (here set out the description). And praying that partition thereof be made among said parties. 

Whereupon the cause was so proceeded in that partition was decreed and the cause referred to Andrew Lang, Esq., as master, to make partition of the land, &c., and the master having made report that the land could not be divided without prejudice, &c., and having valued, and appraised the same,

§244 

and the parties having refused to accept the land at the valuation, the court did, on the 27th day of May, A.D. 1939, order that the master make sale thereof at public auction. 

Pursuant to which order the master did, on the fifteenth day of June, A. D. 1939, sell the land to Alfred Cameron for the sum of ten thousand ($10,000) dollars; which sale on the return day thereto made, was on the third day of July, A. D. 

1939, approved and confirmed by the court and adjudged to remain firm and stable forever. 

Now this Indenture Witnesseth, That Andrew Lang, Esq., master of virtue of the power vested in him by the order aforesaid, and in the consideration of the sum of ten thousand ($10,000) dollars, to him in hand paid, the receipt whereof is hereby acknowledged, hath granted, bargained and sold, and by these presents doth grant, bargain and sell unto the said Alfred Cameron, his heirs and assigns, the tract of land hereinbefore described, together with all and singular the rights, privileges, hereditaments and appurtenances thereunto, belonging or in any wise appertaining: To have and to hold the same for such estate as the parties named in said bill of complaint as plaintiff and defendants had therein at the time of the filing of said bill.

In witness whereof the said master hath hereunto set his hand and seal, this tenth day of July, A. D. 1939.

ANDREW LANG. (Seal.) Master.

§

637 

FORMS. 

255. Deed of Extinguishment or Assignment of Ground Rent. 

For Form see §116, Page 304. 

The same form is used for extinguishment as for assignment. If the ground rent is assigned to a stranger it is a deed of assignment. If it is assigned to the terre tenant i.e. the owner of the land, the deed of assignment becomes one of extinguishment.

LEASES 256. Lease for Property in City (All Waivers) with Surety Agreement. 

THIS AGREEMENT WITNESSETH, That ROBERT STONE, Agt. 

for Allen Smith, hereinafter called the Lessor, does hereby let unto WILLIAM SLOAN, hereinafter called the Lessee Premises No. 1321 X Street in the City of Philadelphia to be used as dwelling house and for no other purpose, for the term of one year to begin on the Twelfth day of February, 1940, at the rent of FOUR HUNDRED AND EIGHTY ($480) dollars per annum payable in equal monthly payments of Forty ($40) dollars in advance on the Twelfth day of each month at 102 W. St. or at such other place within Philadelphia as said Lessor may in writing from time to time direct; and the said Lessee accepts possession of the said premises upon the said terms, and further agrees to the following covenants, stipulations and conditions: 

1st. Lessee will pay the said specified rent, at the times and in the manner above provided, and all damages, costs and charges in this lease provided for, and in case of non-payment of said rent, damages, costs and charges, if any, or in case the leased premises shall be deserted or vacated, Lessor may enter the same, either by force or otherwise, without being liable to any prosecution or action therefor, and may distrain not only for the specific rent above mentioned, but also for all damages, costs, and charges in this lease mentioned and provided for with the same force and effect as if the same were a distress for rent in arrear, and also relet the same premises, as agent of Lessee for any unexpired portion of the term

$256 

and receive the rent therefor. If Lessee shall, at any time during the continuance of this lease attempt to remove or manifest an intention to remove the goods and chattels out or from said premises or desert or vacate said premises without having paid and satisfied the said Lessor in full for all rent which shall become due during the term of this lease, or any continuation thereof, or if said Lessee becomes embarrassed or makes an assignment for the benefit of creditors, or is sold out by any sale under process of law, then in such cases the whole rent for the whole term of this lease, or any continuation thereof, shall be taken to be due and payable forthwith, and the said Lessor may proceed to distrain for and collect the whole in the same manner as if, by the conditions of this lease, the rent for the whole term, or any continuation thereof, were payable in advance, any law, usage of custom to the contrary notwithstanding. Lessee also agrees that all property on the said premises shall be liable to distress for rent, and for said damages, costs and charges, and for all costs of distress, watchmen's wages and constables' commissions, including such as may, by Act of Assembly, be chargeable to Lessor ; and in case any goods shall have been removed from the leased premises, Lessor may follow, take and return said goods to the leased premises, or distrain on and sell the same wherever found. Lessee hereby waiving the benefit of all laws made or to be made exempting property from levy and sale, either on distress for said rent, damages, costs, and charges, or on a judgment for said rent, damages, costs and charges, or for breach of any other of the conditions herein contained. 

2d. Lessee shall not use, or allow to be used, the said premises for any purpose other than above mentioned, nor assign the said lease, nor underlet the said premises, or any part thereof, without the written consent of Lessor endorsed hereon. This covenant shall apply as well to an involuntary transfer by operation of law, whether by execution, insolvency or bankruptcy, as to a voluntary assignment or underletting.

$639 

FORMS. 

3d. Lessee will, during the term remove or cause to be removed from said premises any and all ashes, rubbish or refuse matter and keep, and at the expiration thereof deliver up, the said premises in as good order and condition as the same now are, reasonable wear and tear, and damage by fire or other casualty, not occurring through Lessee's negligence, excepted. Lessee shall not make any alterations, additions, or improvements without Lessor's written consent endorsed hereon, and all alterations, additions or improvements made by either of the parties hereto upon the premises, shall be the property of Lessor and shall remain upon, and be surrendered with, the premises at the termination of this lease, without molestation or injury. 

4th. Unless either party hereto shall give to the other written notice for removal at least Three months prior to the end of said term, this lease shall continue, upon the terms and conditions then in force, for a further period of One year and so on from year to year until terminated by either party hereto giving to the other at least three months written notice for removal prior to the expiration of the then current term; 

Provided, however, that if Lessor shall have given three months written notice, to be served by leaving the same on demised premises previous to the expiration of said term, or of any subsequent term created under the provisions hereof, of Lessor's intention to change the terms and conditions of this lease, and Lessee shall hold over at the end of the then current term after such notice, Lessee shall be considered a tenant under the terms, remedies and conditions of this lease as modified by such notice for a further period of one year and thereafter, from year to year until terminated as hereinabove provided, with the same effect as if a new lease in such form had been duly executed. 

5th. It is further agreed that if the said rent shall at any time be in arrear and unpaid, or if Lessee shall fail to comply with any other of the covenants, terms or conditions of this lease during the said term, or any subsequent term, or with any notice given under the terms hereof this lease may,

at the option of Lessor be forthwith terminated, and thereupon any Attorney may immediately thereafter, as Attorney for Lessee at the request of Lessor sign an agreement for entering in any competent court an amicable action and judgment in ejectment to any term then past or present (without any stay of execution or appeal) against Lessee and all persons claiming under Lessee for the recovery by Lessor of possession of the hereby demised premises, and for all arrearages of rent, if any, without any liability on the part of the said Attorney, for which this shall be a sufficient warrant; and thereupon a writ of habere facias possessionem, with a clause of fieri facias for such arrearages of rent, if any may issue forthwith, without any prior writ or proceeding whatsoever, and Lessee hereby releases to Lessor all errors and defects whatsoever in entering such action on judgment, or causing such writ of habere facias possessionem to be issued, or in any proceeding thereon or concerning the same; and hereby agrees that no writ of error or objection or exception shall be made or taken thereto; and a copy of this lease, with any modifications thereof, being filed in said action, it shall not be necessary to file the original as a warrant of Attorney, any law or rule of court to the contrary notwithstanding. No such determination of this lease, nor taking or recovering possession of the premises, shall deprive Lessor of any other action against Lessee for possession, for rent, or for damages, nor shall any distress or suit for rent or damages prevent Lessor from proceeding to recover possession on a breach of any of the terms or conditions hereof. 

6th. It is hereby further agreed that if Lessor should for any cause be unable to deliver possession of said premises to Lessee at the beginning of the term of this lease or within ten days thereafter, Lessor shall not be liable therefor in damages to Lessee and upon Lessor's being unable to deliver possession before the expiration of said ten days, this lease may at the option of Lessee be forthwith terminated. WITNESSETH, That the said party, in consideration of the rents and covenants hereinafter mentioned, does demise and lease unto the said second party to be used as a Grocery Store the premises situate in the City of Reading, County of Berks and State of Pennsylvania, described as follows: No. 2342 

M. Street.

TO HAVE AND TO HOLD unto the said second party, subject to the conditions of this agreement for the term beginning on the first day of September, 1939, and ending on the 31st day of 

August, 1940.

IN CONSIDERATION OF WHICH the said second party agrees that he will pay to the said first party for the use of said

§257 

1 premises, the sum of Three Hundred and Sixty Dollars, payable as follows viz.: Thirty dollars on the first day of each and every month in advance and the said second party also agrees that he will keep said premises in as good repair and condition as at present and will at the expiration of this lease, surrender up same in like repair and condition, natural wear and damage by the elements excepted; that he will permit no unlawful business to be carried on upon said premises or permit anything to be done contrary to the conditions of the policies of insurance on said premises whereby the hazard might be increased or the insurance invalidated; that he will not underlet said premises, nor assign this lease or any interest therein to any person without the written consent of the said first party; that he will not remove from said premises during the term of this lease without the written consent of the said first party and in case of removal, or attempt to remove, the entire rent reserved for the full term of this lease shall become due and payable at once and may forthwith be collected by distress or otherwise.

That he will keep the said premises in a clean and sanitary condition and remove all ashes or other garbage which may accumulate upon the same during the said term or failing therein pay to the said first party double the cost of removing the same to be recovered the same as rent due and in arrears; that he will pay the rent for the use of water and lights upon the said premises during the said term and which may be recovered by said first party as rent due and in arrears; and the said first party reserves the right to display a "for rent or sale" card upon the said premises and to enter the premises for the purpose of making necessary repairs, or to show the same to prospective purchasers or lessees. 

And any goods removed from said premises, either before or after the expiration of the said term, while any portion of the said rent remains unpaid, whether due or not, shall remain liable to distress for such rent for the period of thirty days after such removal, the same as though they remained upon the premises; and any removal of the goods from the said

$643 

FORMS. 

premises at any time, either by day or by night, without the written consent of the said first party, shall be considered a clandestine and fraudulent removal. And if default shall be made in the payment of any part of said rent for five days after the same becomes due, or if the second party shall break or evade, or attempt to break or evade, any of the covenants, agreements and conditions of this lease, the first party may forfeit and annul the unexpired portion of this lease and enter upon and repossess the said premises without process of law and without any notice whatsoever. 

And it is further agreed that the acceptance by said first party of any of the said rent at any time after the same has become due, or default has been made in the payment thereof or any failure of the first party to enforce any of his rights under this lease or any of the penalties, forfeitures or conditions herein contained, shall not in any wise be considered a waiver of his right to enforce the same and that he may enforce such forfeiture without any notice whatsoever, and that any attempt to collect the rent by one proceeding shall not be considered as a waiver of the right of said first party to collect the same by any other proceeding. 

And the said second party hereby waives the usual three months' notice to quit, and agrees to surrender said premises at the expiration of said term, or the termination of this lease without any notice whatsoever. And upon any proceeding instituted for the recovery of said rent, either by distress or otherwise, the said second party waives the benefit of all appraisement, stay and exemption laws, the right of inquisition on real estate, and all bankrupt or insolvent laws now in force or hereafter passed. 

If default shall be made in the payment of any rent when the same shall become due or if the second party shall permit and judgment to be entered against him or make an assignment for the benefit of creditors, or commit any other Act of Bankruptcy, the rent for the full term shall become immediately due and collectible by distress or otherwise.

§657 

FORMS. 

at least three months prior thereto, but, in default of such notice, this lease shall continue upon the same terms and conditions as are herein contained for a further period of one year, and so on from year to year until determined by either party hereto giving to the other three months' written notice for removal previous to the expiration of the then current term of this lease; provided, however, if the Lessor shall have given such written notice prior to the expiration of any term hereby created of his intention to change the terms and conditions of this lease, and the Lessee shall hold over after the expiration of the time mentioned in such notice, Lessee shall be considered Lessee under the terms and conditions as mentioned in such notice for such further period as Lessee may remain in possession of the premises. If the Lessee shall remove from the premises during the said term, or any renewal thereof, or cease to use or occupy the same as herein provided, the Lessor in Lessor's discretion may immediately and without liability therefor re-enter said premises by force or otherwise, without notice or demand, and lease the said premises to any other person or persons, and the said Lessee shall be liable for any loss in the rent for the balance of the then current term. And it is hereby further agreed that leaving written notice upon said demised premises shall be considered a sufficient service on the said Lessee of said notice. 

31. It is understood that the within lease constitutes the sole agreement and contains all the terms, conditions and provisions thereof by and between the parties hereto and that there are no understandings or oral promises or stipulations by and between the parties hereto except as herein contained. 

32. The Lessee agrees to pay as additional rent hereunder in the event that its annual gross sales during any lease year, shall exceed Thirty-six Thousand ($36,000.) Dollars, 10% upon such excess of gross sales during any such lease year, and the same shall be payable in the following manner, to wit: The Lessee shall on the 10th day of each and every month give to the Lessors a written statement showing the gross

$258 

receipts or sales for the preceding calendar month, and quarter-annually during every such lease year the Lessee shall send with the quarter-annual statement a sum equal to 10% of so much of the gross receipts for such quarter-annual period as may be in excess of Nine Thousand ($9,000.) Dollars; provided, however, that if it shall appear at the end of any lease year that the amount of additional rental paid or payable during such lease year is or shall be more than a sum equal to 10% of gross sales in excess of Thirty-six Thousand ($36,000.) Dollars, the Lessor covenant and agree upon demand of the Lessee to refund to the Lessee such sums as the Lessee shall have paid in excess of 10%, or the Lessee at his option may deduct such sums as he may be entitled to have refunded to him, in whole or part, from any instalment of rent or additional rent then or thereafter due and payable. It is the intention hereof to pay the Lessors in each lease year a minimum annual rental of Thirty-six Hundred ($3,600.) 

Dollars, and after the first year of the term an additional rental which shall not be more than 10% of the gross sales in excess of Thirty-six Thousand ($36,000.) Dollars per annum.

Nothing, however, herein contained shall be deemed to confer upon the Lessors any interest in the business of the Lessee in the demised premises. 

The Lessee further covenants that for the purpose of ascertaining the amount to be paid as additional rent for every quarter as hereinbefore provided, he shall keep at the demised premises books and records which shall show daily sales made by the Lessee in, on or from the demised premises, and further agrees to deliver to the Lessors monthly statements of the total sales made in, on or from said premises, sworn to by Lessee, or at the election of the Lessors give the Lessors access to such books, accounts, records and reports of receipts kept in or upon the premises herein demised, as show the daily sales in such business by the Lessee, and to permit the Lessors to have an audit made thereof by an accountant appointed by the Lessors. If such audit reveals error as the result of which Lessors have been paid less than the amount to which they are entitled, the expense of such

$659 

FORMS. 

audit shall be borne by the Lessee, otherwise it will be borne by the Lessors. If the Lessors are not satisfied with any daily, weekly, annual or other statement rendered to them by the Lessee they shall within ten (10) days of the receipt of such statement give notice in writing to the Lessee of their dissatisfaction. Unless within ten (10) days of the receipt of such notice the Lessee shall have satisfied the Lessors as to such statement, the Lessor shall be entitled to an audit of the books and accounts of the Lessee with respect to sales in demised premises, to be made by the Lessor's accountant and at the joint expense of the Lessor and Lessee. The result of such audit shall be final and binding upon both parties, except in the case of fraud.

The Lessee covenants for the purpose hereof he will install and maintain a system by which will be recorded the daily receipts from all sales and other transactions had in or upon said premises in connection with his business, and that he will keep the same on file for a period of not less than one year, and will give the Lessor and his agents the opportunity to make or inspect and examine such records of receipts, and records of daily deposits of sales in the demised premises, and that he will freely render his own assistance in the making of such examination, inspection or audit, and will, if requested by the Lessors, supply a copy of and authorize the Lessor to examine any Income Tax Return filed by the Lessee during the term of this lease.

 259. Lease Fire Clause.¹ 

In case the building of which demised premises form a part shall be so damaged by fire or other casualty or happening as to substantially destroy the same, then this lease shall cease and come to an end, and any unearned rent paid in advance by the Lessee shall be returned to it; but in case of such damage or destruction of the demised premises as shall not substantially destroy the building, then the demised premises shall be restored to their condition immediately prior to such damage or destruction with due diligence by the Lessor, and a just proportion of the rent herein before reserved, according to the extent of the injury or damage sustained by the demised premises, shall be suspended and abated until the demised premises shall have been so restored and put in proper condition for use and occupation.
